Son: "That's it—you've pounded the life out of me, father, an' now you don't know what you done it for !" MYSTERY EXPLAINED.—" Why is Miss B. wear- ing black ?" "She is in mourning for her husband." "Why, she never had a husband.'' "That is why she mourns. She is grieving over the husband she hasn't got. "True happiness, my son, consists in finding that you have paid two shillings for an article exactly like that for which your friend had to pay half a crown the day before.' SOMEONE is always wanting special legislation. Here comes a man advocating the passing of a law competing railway restaurant-keepers to date their apple pies and sandwiches. "OH, for some new-coined name by which to call him ! Oh, for some name no other lips could give I" was the prayer of Violet until she married him. Now she is content to call him Old Beeswax. "I'm very tired," said the lady at the head of the supper table one Sunday evening. Carruirr NAPPING.—UncIe : "Dear me, Karl, what a poor memory you have." Nephew: "A poor memory, you say? Why, I can repeat four pages of the names in the directory after reading them through only once!" Uncle: "I'll bet you a hamper of champagne that you can't do it." The nephew sends for a directory, attentively peruses tour pages, and shuts up the book. Uncle: "Well?" Nephew: "Smith, Smith, Smith," etc., ad in,finitum. All the four pages of the direc- tory being taken up with this familiar patronymic, our student won his bet in tine style. TEN persons at an hotel table ordered " blanc mange' for dessert. No. 1 called for blammange, No. 2 for blue monge, No. 3 for blanc mange, No. 4 for blong mong, No. 5 for bla mong, Na. 6 for bluemong, No. 7 for blanche mange, No. 8 for blammong, No. 9 for blang maunch, and No. 10 for blammonge. A MAN was travelling lately while afflicted with a very bad cough. He annoyed his fellow travellers greatly, till finally one of them remarked, in a tone of displeasure :—" Sir, that is a very had cough of yours !" "True, sir." replied our friend; "but you will excuse me, it's the best I've got." "My dear, look down below," said Mr. Grand- iose, as he stood on the bridge with his wife, and gazed at a tug hauling a long line of barges. "Such is life : the tug is like the man, working and toiling, while the barges, like women, are— " I know," interrupted Mcs. G., acridly; "the tug does all the blowing, and the barges bear all the burden." THE WAITER'S MISTAKE.—He had made a hearty meal at a restaurant, and, getting up, he said to the waiter: "1 declare if I haven t forgotten my purse !" The waiter fired up, and hurled big words at him for fully three minutes before pausing for breath. When a change came the stranger continued, "But I have a five-pound note here in my waistcoat pocket 1" The waiter could not smile to save his life.

"Well," she answered, "of course it wouldn't be proper for me to ovinpliment myself. But some of the neighlriurs have told me that they have stayed awak,i at night for hours listening to my plriying..' And she smiled in 1, elf-approving way that was sweet to behold. " CAPITAL " CHAT.

The story told by Lord Tweedirouth on Monday about the funds of the National Lifeboat Institu- tion is disheartening enough The country is proud of the service as a whole, and the institution un- questionably deserves support. But its local management is often the subject of complaint, and on two or three occasions lately there have been just animadversions on the conduct of some of the crews. Still, it is difficult to think these things have affected the receipts so much as to reduce the income from £BO,OOO to .C 73,000 a year.

General Booth's arrangement for his forthcoming tour round the world have now been definitely made. He is to leave this country on August 10 h for South Africa' and from South Africa he will pro- ceed to New Zealand, Tasmania, and Australia. He will come home by India, where he proposes to conduct a campaign of nearly seven weeks. Be hopes to land in England on his return about the second or third week of March. Cotr missioner Pollard is to accompany the General on his tour.

--- The hot weather is bringing on the harvest very fast in the southern counties. The prospect is not good. Straw is short, and the ears are small, and the grain will be shrivelled. The bay crop has mostly been secured in fine condition. Hops are suffering from the beat. The pastures are becom- ing very bare, and a great scarcity of keep is im- minent. In the London markets all sorts of produce have been cheap. Potatoes are very plentiful and good. Strawberries are nearly over, but English cherries are abundant and -very good. Salmon is very cheap and good. Bush fruits are very plenti- ful. A few early plums are to hand. The prospects of the English orchards are very good, especially for stone-fruit.

Grouse shooting in Scotland this year will claim additional notice from the fact that the Emperor William is to have a week on the moors. For the rest, however, the reassembling of Parliament is likely to interfere somewhat with the sport, by keeping legislators in the south. This may give the birds in Some parts a somewhat longer reprieve,and this, having regard.to the severity of last winter, will not come amiss. On the highest ranges of the Scottish moors it is doubtful indeed if the winter has left birds enough to make anything like fair sport.

Mr Henry Matthews is fairly well off, and, being unmarried, is in a position to contemplate the ex- penises of a Peerage with equanimity. As Home Secretary for six years be drew thirty thousand pounds, which proved far and away beyond his re- quirements. the new Peer's heir presumptive is his nephew, who is M. de La Chere, of Paris.

Baron James, not Baron Aylestone of Hereford, I as it is said Sir Henry James intends to style him- self,has an important piece of judicial patronave to dispose of almost immediately on his appointment to the Chancellorship of the Duchy of Lancaster. The appointment is the Vice-Chancellorship of the County Palatine of Lancaster. which has become vacant by the death of Mr. F. 0. Robinson; Q.C. It is worth £3,500 a year.

It is not generally known that there are at pre-] sent studying at the Royal Military school of Music Kneller Hall, no fewer than twelve men and boys of the Gold Coast Constabulary, who are being pre- pared to become bandsmen and trainers in Africa. They are all negroes. Although • they speak but little English, they fraternise well with the lads of British regiments under training, and join gladly in most games, such as cricket, football, and lawn tennis. Their progress in their studies is compara- tively slow, but in their capacity for hard work they fairly put their British comrades to shame. This is the first time that PO many have been to- r/ether, though for many years coloured bondsmen have been trained at Kneller Hall for the West Indian regiments, which are also permitted to have , European trained bandmasters.

It is now frankly admitted among the Unionists that their successes have not been in any sense due to the " programme "put. forward by Lord Salis- bury on the day of the prorogation. The old-age pensions scheme is still relied upon to have an 4-ffret in the counties, hut in London at all events it has done nothing for the candidates. Two things have bren mainly trusted to—the badness of trade and the unpopularity of the Local Veto Bill, in respect to the provisions of which the most extra- ordinary misrepresentations have been current. It seems to have been absolutely impossible to drive I it out of the heads of a section of voters that the I passing rf the measure meant the indiscriminate closing of the public-houses, while the " rirhmen's clubs" were to remain open. This suggestion has I been used with the utmost ingenuity by the Union- I ists canvasser. I am told that certain of the Liberal candidates attribute their defeat, in part, to the quarrels which have taken place among the members of the Irish party; but although some votes may have been lost in this way,there is reason to believe that, generally speaking. the Irish ques- tion has not been very prominent. The Church vote has undoubtedly been influential, but it has been given without any great demonstration. Local Veto, had trade, and 66 Independent Labour " are at the bottom of the Liberal reverses in the capital, and to these may be adde I the unfortunate fact that n number of constituencies were nnprovided with Liberal candidates up to the eleventh hour.

There is one thing for which this election wilt he memorable, and that is the uncertainty which up to the last has been felt even among experts as to the turn which the election was going; to take. For in,-tance. in North Lambeth even after the poll had closed a lame body of Unionists still believed that Mr. Stanley had been defeated. A great deal of this was no doubt due to the uncertainty as to the strength of the opposition to Local Veto. However, there is the same doubt now in respect to the counties. How the rural constituencies are going 'no one can pretend to foretell. They may yet redeem the Liberal pot,ition, but none of the experts on either side with whom I have an opportunity of talking profess much confidence in the matter. The indications from the smaller boroughs are not par- ticularly trustworthy. Take, for instance, the Eastern counties. There were two or three "index "constituencies among those which polled on Monday. but the In.s nt Boston was as unloolted for as the gain at Grimsby.

Sir William Harcourt, far from being depressed by his defeat, takes it smilingly, and is in the best of spirits. After the result at Derby,some of his friends and relatives pressed him to retire, but he would not hear of it. Of course he has been over- whelmed by offers of seats each described as "per- fectly safe if you would fight it," but naturally this sort of thing does not tempt him. Mr. Gladstone wrote him a long and sffeetionate letter of sym- pathy at once, and so. curiously enough, did the Duke of Devonshire. Mr.Balfoor has also expressed indirectly his great regret. Sir William Harcourt himself says he has only one regret in resigning office at this moment—namely,that he has left such a very large and rapidly growing surplus in the National Exchequer to tim Unionist party,who will, of course, not fail to point to it as the immediate result of the change of Government.

The scene of enthtigagm at the National Liberal Club when Mr. Keir rdie's defeat was announced WIIP of a significant character. The great meeting room rang with cheerino.which was renewed when the secretary, Mr. Donald Murray, placed in the corner of the placard not the black disc announcing a Liberal defeat, but the red disc, which is used to denote a Liberal gain.

The Japanese have but barely had breathing time in which to recover from the effects of their struggle with China.when they are threatened with another invasion This time it is General Booth who has his eye upon Japan, and who bas resolved upon planting the flag of the Salvation Army upon its shores. The G.neral has made the preparations for taking the field and for commencing operations immediately. The first contingent starts for Japan under the leadership of Colonel Wright. The meet- ing held in the City Temple to inaugurate the pro- ceedings was of a most enthusiastic character, and should be a happy augury for the future of the work of the Army in Japan.

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAREST NELLIE,

I daresay you found out long ago how dowdy last year's skirts look if we try to make them up for this year. The cut of this year's skirt differs very much, and I am giving you a design of one of the new shapes which is suitable for most occasions. It may be carried out in any material, from the richest satin to the humblest cotton. The front is plain, cut out of the double material with a straight edge of the pattern laid on the fold. The entire skirt consists of five gored breadths, of which four form the side and back pieces, and the fifth the front. The skirt requires to be interlined, preferably with crin, the favourite French material for the purpose, a stuff composed, as its name implies, in great part of horsehair. Though this is dearer than ordinary stiffening, costing about Is. 3d. a yard, it has the advantage of being durable, elastic, and retaining its shape. All skirts of silk and material now being made in France on our model have two fine steels inserted in front a little above the hem, to ensure the skirt setting out in the orthodox semi-eircular fashion. Many English-cut skirts have an ugly trick of kicking up right in the centre of the front breadth, and this has a very ungraceful effect, sometimes obviated by raising the skirt a little on the hip towards the back, which may throw in the front somewhat. The organ-pipe pleatings at the back require to be held in place by elastic, and on the position of this elastic, varying with the require- ments of each figure, much of the success depends. The lining chosen to cover the crin may be of thin silk or sateen, of matching or contrasting hue ; and a frill of silk to match the lining is usually added as a balayeuse, round the hems, though this may be omitted. In France the favourite lining is a material called polonaise, a soft mixture of cotton and silk, to be had in every possible shade —shot, or striped, or plaid; as preferred. Blouses are now being worn in Paris with short sleeves. A fashionable one was made in white Pongee silk, the sleeves, which reached to the elbow, having double puffs. The yoke was trimmed with jet, and down the front, which was box-pleated, were jet buckles. Long black silk gloves reaching to the elbow were worn. The toque was trimmed with short silk and blue pansies, the skirt being of the black crepon.

Some of the Regent-street giillinery is something to be seen. The following hat is not only one that every woman should want to wear, but a hat that nearly every woman can wear; for it is wonder- fully cheap. Such an excellent hat it is, too. It is made of Panama trimmed with white wings and black satin ribbon ; and equally cheap is one which turns up at one side, and is made of straw, trimmed with white satin set into points. Exceeding good it is, fit to grace a garden party, always supposing it is set on a fair, young face. It needs a fair, young face. But she who possesses not that luxury may be advised to indulge herself with that brown straw, with a trimming of black silk-edged accor- dion-kilted bows clasped with diamond buckles. At one side stands erect a spray of yellow flowers deepening into orange, and just in the front are two black wings. At the same shop was a really capital sailor hat, of a coarse straw. It has a good brim and a good crown; it is known as the Club sailor, and it costs 6s. 6d., and, as the Irishwoman would say, "Here's long life to it !" It is trimmed with a large bow of satin ribbon and two wings.

The collar or yoke of embroidered cambric or lace and muslin has come assertively to the front rank in the summer fashions, and the newest are composed of white sprigged net or muslin trimmed with lace and insertion in buttercup-yellow tint, others by contrast being tinted in golden-tan or ecru d'or, but the combined attractions of the white and yellow are decidedly the prettier. These collars are in every instance edged with frills of lace or embroidery, the outlines varying either in three points, square, or umbrella-shaped with six points, and mostly fastened at the back, as also do the blouses, one of which I must describe. A white muslin closely powdered over with oblopg dots had been chosen, and was made full into a yoke, over which an extremely pretty collarette was arranged in plain muslin, made quite round with a deep frill edged with a narrow Valenciennes lace in yellow and two rows of insertion, the fulness con- tained up the throat in a series of little tucks drawn full and each one edged with a similar row of narrow lace. And now I will tell you of a coat of a very little boy. It is a promenade coat, and. is suitable for little boys-from 2 years to 4 years, and is made in

arranged

cream Bengaline trimmed silk guipure. it could also be made in cream fancy cloth. A white satin three-cornered hat with mount of three-quartat ostrich tips is worn with this coat. Your loving JENsza.

A greater number of men than women become stout late in life. No satisfactory explanation is offered of this fact.

It is pleasant. to taste ; it produces natural quiet sleep, by relieving the child from pain, swift e little cherub awakpe "as bright as a button." Of al, chemists, is. lid. per bottle.—Advt.

INER, SATURDAY, J ULY 20. 1895.

It takes seventy thousand insects to make one pound of cochineal. To detach a fish-bone from the throat, swallow a raw egg as quickly as it can be obtained. Ink-stains on anything white can be removed by wash- ing first in strong salt and water and then in lemon-juice. This does not rot the fibres like salts of lemon. Paris actresses wear paper lace, which by night looks as beautiful and delicate as the best of real lace, while it costs but a trifle. Each figure in a first-class waxworks exhibition has to be re-made every four or five years. The wax loses colour, and becomes soft and spongy after that time. There are now in the United Kingdom one hundred land seventy-seven women doctors and one hundred and twenty women members of School Boards. Le Bing Nam, a Chinese Athlete, says that his extra- ordinary strength is due to his diet,which consists chiefly of boiled rice and boiled ducks' heads. The brains of the duck, he asserts, are very strengthening. A match-cutting machine is quite an automatic curiosity. It cuts ten million sticks a day, and then arranges them over a vat, where the heads are put on at a surprising rate of speed. Taken altogether, the value of the fish landed on the coast of England and Wales for the first two months of the preaent year was seven hundred and twenty-seven thousand pounds as compared with seven hundred and sixty-six thousand six hundred and eighty pounds for the equivalent period of 1894. The Queen received from the Nizam of Hyderabad, premier Prince of India, a birthday gift, of which there has enly just been any public mention. It is a Life of Her Majesty written, in Hindustani, on the figure of a tree. It is called .K/rat guitar ; which,being interpreted, means" the style of flower." The penmanship, which is beautiful, was a year's work. When Queen Victoria was first photographed by Mr. Down* y,says the" Mascot," he was asked by his friends "What did you say ? " and " What did she say ? " " Well," said Mr. Downey, " I took Her Majesty just as I wad anny ither pairson ; and when I'd settled her I said" Wad it please Her Majesty to put on a more fav- ourable countenance al ' and she said, Sairtainly, Mr. Dooney. ' " The University of Durham, which has done so much for men who could not afford to go to Oxford or Cam- bridge, has opened its doors to women students. Vi ith the exception of Divinity, all the Durham degrees are now open to women. At a Convocation last week the degree of B.Sc. was conferred on Miss Ella Mary Bryant, of the Durham College of Science, Newcastle-on-Tyne. Miss Bryant is the first woman to obtain a degree under the new Charter. Her presentation by the Sub-Warden at the Convocation created great enthusiasm. Paris takes the palm as regards the luxury rife among her gilded celebrities. La Bella Jeanette. 3 lady of his- toric fame, has thus arranged her bath-room. To begin I with, the light falls through a large skylight of dark blue panes. The bath itself is in pink crystal, encircled with silver, with steps of the same metal. Further, it is draped with costly Malines lace. In her own room exotics pre- dominated. The Alergon lace curtains of her be i are caught up with bunches of lace flowers, while garlands of the same trim the head of her bedstead. The Use of Tears.

The use of tears is to cleanse the eye. They act as a tonic, keeping the eye soft and bright. The reason why some people cry more easily than others is on account of a more delicate nervous system, which causes the nerve fibres about the tear glands te vibrate more easily, re- sulting in a flow of tears. Although tears do not injure the eves, they inflame the skin, and it is only the hero- ines of novels who can weep without disfiguring them- selves.

Chinese Girls.

In Yanan, in China, girl children are sold into slavery to a dreadful extent. In one year, says Dr. Morrison, no less than three thousand children, chiefly girls, were sold to dealers, and carried like poultry in baskets to the capital. In or iinary times the price for girls is 34. for every year of their age, but in time of famine chil- dren become a drug in the marker, and little girls are sold at from 3s. 4d. to Qs. each. They are bought in the capital for wives and " gatows," but are rarely sold into prostitution. The price for a wife varies according to her beauty, the chief item being the smallness of her feet.

Summer Complaints. Diarrhcea is a very common disease in the summer- time. Cholera is nothing more than exaggerated diarrhcea. When a man is said to have died of diarrhcea he has really died of cholera. It may be well to know that the first, the mot important, and the most indis- pensable item in the arrest and cure of looseness of the bowels is absolute quietude on a bed. Nature hers,-If always prompts this by disinclining us to locomol ion The next thing is to eat nothing but common rice, parched like coffee, and then boiled, and taken with a little salt and butter, Drink little or no liquid of any kind. Bits of ice may be eaten and swallowed at will. Every step taken, every spoonful of liquid, only aggra- vates the disease.

Alone and Unprotected. To any woman who meditates a solitary cycling Our. Miss N. U. Bacon's experiences, related in the " Lady Cyclist," will come as a comfort. " Fcr two consecutive summers," sirs she, "I have toured alone and unpro- tected, and no one has ever treated me discourteou,ly or unkindly. N.;t only so, but I have often accompanied one or two more lady riders in moonlight rides, and havo never suffered any discomfort or grievance.. Whilst I was touring the year Islore last the miners' strike" (lock-out it should be)" filled the country with terror and dismay; but although I met groups of most forbid- dxig who had gone to the woods to eat berries and nuts to satisfy their craving for food, no one showed any signs of ii tnrferimg with me, but, nn the contrary, as if ti reassure me of their friendly attitude, they raisod their hats and said good morning to me as I cycled past."

Quite a number of women now-a-days possessing Far- dens earn money by disposing of the surplus produce. They have discovered there is a market for flower blos• 130111 e. seeds, seedling plants and vegetables. It may not be a large amount which they are enabled to bring in, but quite sufficient to provide them with neressai pocket money. A lady of limited means residing in the country laughs and says her garden clothes herself and daughter. In other words she sePii enough out of her garden to buy all their clothes. Another lady half for- nishes her house by this means, and many o hers might be mentioned who by the aid of the gardens have brought in a welcome addition to a meagre income. Many women supply their friends in town or those whose gard, n ground is one limited scale ; while others find a shot,. cheap advertisement in one or two of the newspapers brim- s them cmtomers and enabl 5 them to graduall • buid up a small connection. Of course this is not done without work. One important matter should always be borne in mind—to supply a good article at a reasonable price. This is too often lost sight of by People wanting to find a sale for their produce. WHO IS THE BEST AN GLER ?

--- Mr. William Sonior—" Red Spinner "—writinr, in the V indaor Magazine and dealing w'th " Angling rs an Exercise " remark; :—" Trout fishit"., is the sport of the many amongst fly-fishermen, and the exercise required in the methods which are recognised as quite orthodox is probably the happy medium, yielding pleasure with the least penalty of toil. The members of the most recent school of trout fishers are believers in the floating fly, but it is wrong to assume that there is any burning question in the matter. The hest angler is the man who is master of all the legiti- mate devices for beguiling fish into his landing net, and I AM not now concerned with any controversial as- pects of the dry-fly question. The spectacle of an angler upon a chalk stream, where this style is to all intents and purposes Hobson's choize, is not at all suggestive of bodily activity should he happen to be waiting for a rise.' IThe trout will only heed an artificial fly that is dropped in front of them with upstanding wings: and in form of body and appendages, as in the manner of its progress on the surface of the stream, this enunter- feit presentment most strictly imitate the small smile. merie which are hatching in the bed and floating down the surface of the stream. As the trout do not rise un- til the natural fly appears, and as the hatches of fly are capricious, there are often weary hours of waiting when the angler must he perforce inactive. Pis exercise comes in full measure when the hour of action does ar- rive, and he will find some motion in the eventless in- tervals by walking up the river on the look out for olive ; dun or red quill. The whipper of the mountain streams, lor the wet-fly practitioner who fishes a river where the 1 trout are not particular in their taates, is in the way of exercise the most fortunate of all. He is ever passing from pool to pool, lightly equipped,changing his scenery every hour, now whipping in the shadow of overhang- ing branches, now crouching behind a messy crag, end now brushing the green embroidery of an open section of the stream. The broad tranquil flow is exchanged for merry ripples and sparkling shallows, and these are succeeded by strong and concentrated streams foaming , and eddying adown a rocky gorge. Trout here and there are dronped into the pannier from time to time, end it is a wholesomely tired angler, with a grand ap- petite and capacities for sound Sleep, who at night will ! welcome his slippers at the inn."

GLENALPINE'S OATH; OR, THE MARK OF THE BLACK CROSS.

BY SYLVANUS COBB, 33.

This is a story of love and war, and is full of adventures in the Highlands of Scotland, on the sea, and in the jungles of India. It is intensely interesting from the first to the last.

CHAPTER IV. THE DUEL.

On the morning succeeding the events of the gaming-table, Harold and the two captains met at an early hour, and walked out into the park, where the subject of the late brawl was discussed. The officers desired that the matter should be pushed no further. Not only had Talford deliberately and openly cheated at cards, but he had done it in out- rage of the hospitality of a friend. A duel, they thought, could only serve to give wings to the dis- graceful fact. The were confident that they could bring their comrade around to a friendly adjust-

ment. But Harold had his own ends to answer, and finally he proposed the thing which he wished to accomplish, keeping to himself, however, his ulterior purpcse. His companions listened, and shook their beads; but after a time they withdrew their oppo- sition. Near to the mansion they stopped, and Berwick laid his hands upon the shoulders of the two captains. "1 thank you, gentlemen, for your kind consent," be said. "I wish simply to give the young man a lesson,—nothing more, I assure you. He has brought with him from college a few conceits which can be taken from him without harm." "To please you we will do it," said Lund. "Will you arrange with Talford, or shall I?" "You had better leave that in our hands. You shall hear from us as soon as the lieutenant is up." It was Bashaw who said this, and, as he spoke, Lionel Talford appeared upon the scene. Harold withdrew, leaving the officers to themselves, and ere long afterwards Lundie brought him wood that the thing had been arranged. Arthur was bathing ha head over the waeh-basin when Harold entered his chamber. "Finish your toilet my boy," said the latter, "You've got something important to say to me.

Harold ?" "Yes,—or I should not have intruded in this unseemly fashion. But go on and finish dressing. There is no hurry." Arthur looked into his step-brother's face with an eager wistful expression; but be met only a blank smile in return, and witheut further remark he resumed his dressing, which task was soon accomplished. "Now, Harold—what is it ? " "Are your nerves steady, old fellow?" Arthur coloured with shame. Harold marked the effect of his question, aud quickly added: "I found it necessary to swallow a goodly meas- ure of brandy this morning. I don't think the prescription would be bad for you." "0, what fools we are 1" the youth cried, as he turned towards his dressing-table, on which was an ebony liquor-case. "I wonder if I shall always be thus cursed 1" "I hope not, Arthur." "And I," responded the unhappy victim, after he had swallowed ai:traught of brandy, "can swear ! that I will not 1" He set the glass back upon the table, and then seated himself. His face was flushed, and his blue eyes were devoid of their wonted lustre and the uprising of the veins upon his white brow gave token of pain. "Harold, we gambled last night ?" It was plain from the manner in which he asked the question, that his mind was upon something more weighty beyond, but he took this method of reaching the more important subject. " We had a social game of loo." "And I lost ? " "You? you lost?" repeated Harold, with affected surprise. "What do you mean?" I mean what I say. Dal I not lose at every turn ?" "Do you remember the game?" "No. But I find my pockets empty this morn- ing.. "Pockets empty!" cried Harold. "What in the world are you talking about? You must have been particularly oblivious." "I think," said Arthur with another flush of shame, "that I was drunk enough to be obli- vious." "And yet you won heavily." " Won ? 1 won ?" "Yes." "From whom ?" "From all of us, of course. Why, bless your heart, old fellow, the angel of luck seemed to take you directly under her wing. Where is your pocketbook? You stowed the money in there. I acted as banker, you remember, and redeemed the counters at the end of the game. I think I paid you nearly a thousand pounds." " Nonsense ! The stakes we played for couldn't have run up to such a sum." "The legitimate stakes I admit," said Harold, with a smile; "but you became reckless, as did all of us, and the stakes were doubled and quadrupled; and twice you doubled the quadruple, and won both times." Arthur went to his evening coat, which hung over the back of a chair, and took his book from the breast-pocket thereof, and when he opened it he found it stuffed with bank-notes, together with several pieces of gold. He took out the notes, and ran his fingers over their ends. There was certainly a thousand pounds represented in the figures which caught his eye. "Harold, I wish to Heaven I could give this money back. If I could find out how much I won from each one, I would refund every shilling of

"And thus add insult to ill-luck. Now do you suppose Captain uundie would take the offer?" "And yet, Harold, I'm sorry I won it." "Easy, old fellow. There's something else more deserving of sorrow than that." Arthur clutched his hands, and resumed his seat. "I had trouble with Lionel Telford?" he said, after he had somewhat composed himself. "Yes," replied Harold, with solemn seriousness. And thereupon he went on to give an account of what had transpired, taking care that the colouring should ;tot lack in its darker shades. "I think I remember," said the unhappy youth, "of striking Telford upon the cheek. That one act stands out in sharp relief from the darkness. You say I called him a cheat and a liar ?" Yes." "And did he strike me ?" "No. He did not raise his hand." "Have you seen him this morning?" "He has been out, and I have seen his friends." "Out with it, Harold. Let me know the whole. You have told the worst. Will Telford accept of satisfaction ?" "He demands it." "And he shall have it. Will you serve me ?" "I would rather be shot myself, Arthur, than see you harmed; but I don't see how you can honourably avoid this meeting, and as we must keep it as quiet as possible, I will act for you. I will go and see Captain Lundie, and have the arrangements made at once. Have you any direo- tions to give ?" "None. You know what to do." "This does not take you by surprise, Arthur ?" "No. I have expected it. I remember the blow." 4, Touching weapons—" "Anything. Suit yourself. It is all one to me." "And the time—" "The sooner the better." "Are your nerves steady enough ?" "Pshaw I If I waited until my brain was cleat I might not have heart to fire at the friend whom I had insulted. But go, Harold. I will be ready when you return." When Arthur had been left alone, he paced up and down his chamber until he had reviewed the whole situation as it appeared to him. "I am an unfortunate dog at best," he mut- tered, "and let this thing turn as it may, it must crush me. Sir John loves me not overmuch now, and he will be in a fury if I wound one of his guests. And if I fall? Such a thing may be. In that event I would like to leave his lordship some little token of my gratitude for his fatherly care and kindness; for I know that he has loved me in the other times. I have been foolish, I know; but I have not meant to be wicked. I will leave a line for Sir John that he may know how I feel." He eat down to his table and prepared to write. He filled half a page and tore it up. Then he took a fresh sheet and commenced anew. But he could

satiny numiejr, _r rom be utspratton or ma frank and truthful heart expressions crept upon the paper which, when he read them over, he feared might be construed as the wailings of a suf- fering child, and he tore up the second sheet. He had just done this when Harold returned, and quickly brushing away his writing materials, he started to his feet.

"Come, Harold, be frank and outspoken. Let wie know the whole story in as few words as pos- sible." "I can tell it in a very few words indeed, my boy. Captain Lundie and Talford have gone out to the glade beyond the old chapel, and there we are to join them. I have given the pistols to Bashaw, who is a bit of a surgeon in his way and who will attend us. Further arrangements can be made upon the ground. Now, when you are ready—" "I am ready." Arthur put on his coat and hat, and having taken another swallow of the brandy, he followed Harold from the chamber. They went out by a postern, and nearly a mile i away, n a secluded vale, they found the other party. Captain Bashaw had already arrived and loaded the pistols while Harold and Lundie measured off the ground. Talford stood with his back only ex- posed, and did not look around. Arthur also wished to avoid observation. Once he looked towards the man whom he had stricken, and had he at that moment caught Lionel 'falford's eye what a world of anguish might have been saved. Each would have seen in the other's face that there was no enmicy between them would have seen only shame and regret,—but it was not to be.

At length the arrangements were complete, and Moold explained them to Arthur as he delivered the loaded pistol. "We have marked the distance twelve yards. You will stand back to back—that saves the un- pleasant inclination to stare each other In the fees —and the words will be: Ready I fire I At the word, fire," you turn and discharge your piece as quickly or as deliberately as you please." "I understand," said the youth. "Let it be over as speedily as possible." The men were placed in position and the words given. They both turned at the second command and Telford fired and missed. Then Arthur raised his weapon above his head and discharged it into the air. "Fool " cried Harold, advancing towards him, "you are wasting time and patience. Your act will be taken only as fresh insult." "I have simply signified my heart's desire," re- plied Arthur, quietly. "If Lionel Talford demands another shot I am ready." "He will stand here all day, but he will have a shot from you fairly delivered." "I will not detain him so long. Lam still at his command." Harold conferred with the others, and Arthur was soon informed that the lieutenant demanded another shot. So the pistols were reloaded and the combatants once more placed in position. 'READY I—FIRE 1"

..

At the word Arthur turned and fired, this time with a point-blank range, but without aim. Tal- ford uttered a sharp cry and threw up his arms; and before his second could reach him he had fallen prone upon the sward. "My God 1" uttered Arthur, "I have not shot him ?"

"Wait here and I will see." Stricken with terror and remorse, the young man dropped his pistol and leaned back against a tree. What had he done ?—and what the cause? Twelve short hours ago he and Lionel Talford had been as brothers—and now the blood of his friend was upon his hands. He had insulted his own guest—had stricken him a blow—and now-0 1 the suspense was dreadful 1 He saw them take the limp form of Talford into their arms, and bear it away into the shadow of the thicket. He would have gone to the spot, but Harold met him and detained him. "Arthur, you must think of yourself now 1" "Is he dead ? " "Shot through the heart 1" "0, my God 1" "Bear up,—bear up. It is done, and cannot be undone. I had not thought of such a termina- tion. You know the law, and you know how rigid Sir John is in such matters. It is a bad business." Arthur bowed his head, and made no reply. "What will you do ? " Harold asked, after a pause. "Is Lionel surely dead ? " "I doubt if he knew what hurt him. The bullet went straight through his heart. Bashaw fol- lowed its course with a probe. lam sorry. It is dreadful. Things were bad enough between you and Sir John before. I don't know how he will take this." Arthur groaned in agony of spirit. "If it had been a duel growing out from some fair and honourable cause," pursued Harold, ex- corie.tingly, "there might have been ground for hope of adjustment with his lordship ; but as it is, I fear the worst. He will be apt to see in it only what he will be pleased to term the legitimate result of your dissipated course of life. He will see a drunken brawl over the gaming-table; he will see you intoxicated to beastly insensildlity $ he will see one of his own guests insulted, struck, and then shot dead—and all this at your hand. Arthur, I don't want to advise. You must exercise your own judgment." "Without meaning evil—without a thought of evil against any living thing—l have arrived at this !" groaned the youth. "I cannot go back to the mansion—l cannot !" He walked away a piece and meditated alone. When he came back the uncertain look had given place to an ex.pression of fixed determination. "Harold, can I count upon you to help me in this last strait ?" "You may command me, Arthur." "In helping me you will help yourself." "The only joy I ask you is to serve you, my boy." "Your prospect will be good, Harold, when I am out of the way. "Stop 1" uttered the step-brother, peremptorily. "I will hear none of that. If you think I can be influenced by such motives I will withdraw at once." "Forgive me, Harold. I meant no offence. Serve me, and I will thank you. I cannot go back to Birchmear. I will not see Sir John again. I want my portmanteau and my morocco dressing-case. The case is locked, and I will have it as it is. Into the portmanteau I wish you would put my velvet shooting-jacket, and my wrapper, with a change of linen. And my Spanish cloak—if you would bring that too." " Are you serious, Arthur ?" "1 am serious." "You will go to London for awhile ?" "I will go away from Birchmear." "But you will let me know where I may address you? Who knows but that we may be able to fix this matter up." "We can consider that afterwards. Time presses now. Will you serve me ?" With more protestations of friendship Harold turned away upon his mission, promising that he would bring such things as might be required for a journey. "Bear in mind," suggested our hero, " that I may decide to cross over into France." Harold said he would remember. Within an hour he returned, accompanied by hie own vaht, bringing with him the portmanteau, the dressing-case and the cloak. "I think that I have packed up all that you will require," he said; "and I have also brought Tony along, who will go with you and carry your lug- gage as far as you wish. He understands the whole thing, and you may trust him." Arthur accepted the services of the valet. "You have money enough?" "Yes. My unexpected winnings will serve in. well." "You will write to me, old fellow ? " "When I am settled—yes." "And, meantime, be sure I will do my best to make matters right at Birchmear." The words of—parting were brief, and Harold stood and watched his step-brother until he had disappeared beyond the v ood that skirted the vale. Then a smile of exultation sat grimly upon his dark face and he muttered to himself : "Well played—well played! I think I have the leading hand now; and it must 'tie my own fa-alt if I do not win the game !" lo be continued ).

THE WARRINGTON ELECTION.

RESULT OF THE POLL.

The election of a representative for Warrington in the House of Commons took place on Monday. The poll opened at eight o'clock in the morning and continued until eight o'clock in the evening. Dur- ing the day there was great excitement in the town, and probably at no previous election were so many party colours shown. Notwithstanding this free display of colour, there was very little display of ill-feeling, and both sides exhibited the greatest of good humour. During the day the two midi- dates frequently drove round the town, Mr. Scott being accompanied by Mr. Schwann, M.P , Mr. A. H. Crosfield, and Councillor William Pierpoint. Mr.R. Pierpoint was accompanied by Sir Gilbert Greenall and ;NIL John White. A large number of convey• ances were engaged, the Conservatives having the preponderance. At eight o'clock in the evening, a large crowd commenced to assemble in front of the Town Hall, and by the time the result was an- nounced from the balcony, there were many thou- sands present. To keep down any disorder which might occur during the day, a large body of county ' police had been drafted into the town, but their services were very little called for.

COII-NTING THE VOTES. After the close of the poll, the ballot boxes were conveyed to the Town Hall, where the votes were counted in the Council Chamber. A large number of counters had been engaged and the votes were counted with great despatch, the result being known by 9.25 p.m., a rapidity of counting which ' a highly creditable to the arrangements made by the TOWR Clerk. The result of the election was in doubt until about ten minutes before the end of the counting, and at one time it even appeared proba- ble that Mr. Scott would come out at the head of the poll. A good number of prominent townsmen were in the room during the counting, but neither of the candidates were present. The Mayor pre- sided, and he was assisted by the Town Clerk, and and Mr. J. L. Tunstall, F.C.A. At 925, the Town Clerk announced the result to be Pierpoint 4,001, and Scott 3,326. There were five papers un- accounted for, but neither party wished for a re- count. After the result was announced in the counting room, Mr. WILLIAM PIM:11'01NT, on behalf of the Liberals thanked the Mayor for the able manner in which the poll had been conducted. Their thanks were also due to the Town Clerk and the officials for the care which had been exercised in the election throughout the day, as well as for the kind manner in whi3h the Liberal party had been treated. (Hear, hear.) He concluded by proposing a vote of thanks to the Mayor. Mr. Joari WHITE, on behalf of the Conservatives, seconded the motion most cordially, and remarked that he agreed with all Mr. William Pierpoint had said He congratulated the Mayor and the Town Clerk upon the excellent arrangements they had made for taking the poll. (Hear, hear.) The motion was agreed to, and the Mayor then went to the steps in front of the Town Hall and , read out the result as follows:

Fierpoimt (C) 4OOl Scott(L) 3326

Conservative Majority 675 The result was received with loud cheers inter- mingled with groans, which were repeated again and again. After hearing the result, the crowd, which was an extremely large one, dispersed to the political clubs, and over the town generally and in some streets the excitement, which so tar as we have heard was always good-natured, did not sub- side till well into Tuesday morning. THE POLLING.

The poll was larger in the aggregate but not in pro- portion to last time. At the 1892 election 7,126 voted out of 7,906, leaving only 780 who did not go to the poll. On this occasion, 7,345 voted out of 8,449, leaving 1,104 who did not vote. This gives a percentage of about 86, while the usual percentage is over 90. Below we give a table showing the number of voters on the registers in each ward, the number that voted, the number that did not vote, and, for comparison, the number that did not vote in the same wards in 1892. There were no doubt a geod number of Liberals who refrained from voting owing to their not making an acquaintanceship with Mr. Scott. It is to be hoped that if such be the case, these Liberals will do their duty on the next occasion. Voters. Voted. The Town Clerk on behalf of the Mayor (Councillor James Fairclough), who acted as Returning Officer,made admirable arrangements for carrying out the election, and both parties expressed their entire satisfaction with them.

The number of voters in the Parliamentary Borough is 3,449. The number in 1892 was 7,906, so that there is an increase of 543. After a song had been given by one of the audience, with allusions to Mr. Scott, - Mr. HENRY GREENALL, who was cheered, said his name bad been mentioned that evening. Ile wanted no thanks, and he agreed with them that they had gained all they wanted to gain ; at any rate they had shown the opposite side that they could beat whoever was brought against them. (Applause.) Alderman FeeNcome expressed himself much pleased with the result of the day's work. Mr. C. S. BURGESS saki he congratulated the audience upon the magnificent victory which had been achieved that day, and he rejoiced that he had been allowed to take ever so small a part in the contest. They looked to the Government to give them measures for the ameli- oration of all classes of the community ; they expected that laws would be passed which would be for the bone- fit of all. In conclusion,he wished Mr. Pierpoint health and strength to discharge the responsible duties which had been imposed upon him. Mr. J. GRACE said that the electors had that day proved themselves true to their Queen, their constitu- tion and to themselves. Mr. PIERPOINT was then carried shoulder high across the room and down the steps into the street. He was at once recognised by a large crowd who assembled in the street. and was loudly cheered. Great excitement pre- vailed, and it was with no little difficulty, owing to the alarming rush which took place to get into the Conser- vative Club, that be succeeded in making his way into the second class billi room, where he delivered another speech standing on one of the billiard tables. He thanked the workingmen of Warrington for what they had done for him, and said he regarded himself as the workingmen's representative. They had that day re- turned him by a large majority, and they were a fresh example of those which had already been shown throughout the country. Several speeches were delivered in the first class bil- liard room before Mr. Pierpoint arrived. Councillor Amami BENNETT observed that he was sure he only expressed the general sense of that crowded and enthusiastic meeting when he said how proud they were that they had succeeded once more in returning Mr. Pierpoint and by an increased majority. They had less fear than ever now of losing the election unless a stronger candidate was brought into the field. They must acknow- ledge that their opponent had fought a plucky battle. and had fought it well. (Applause.) Councillor sMETIIIIRST asked the audience to join in singing "God save the Queen." This was done, cheers being given for the successful candidate. When Mr. PIERPOINT entered the room he was loudly cheered. Mounting one of the billiard tables he delivered another speech. Ile said that years ago at a municipal election he thought it his duty as the then Chairman of the Conservative Association to address some hard words to the members of that body, but he was happy to say that since that time the work of the Conservative party had been completed. (Cheers.) To use a Lancashire phrase it had been " jannock." (Hear hear.) The electors had that day returned him te Parliament by a magnificent majority, and if he had done his duty dur- int_t the past three years, and had merited their thanks, he assured them that those thanks had been given most thoroughly and most heartily. He should never forget that night, nor should he ever forget the grand and great work which had been done for the cause which he had the honour to represent. He asked them, as he had done in the other room in the building, to use their influence at other elections which had not yet been decided, and to work for the Unionist candidates— (hear, hear)—so that they might make the tide which was flowing with them flow in greater volume. He hoped that other constituencies would emulate the noble example which had been set by Derby. (Applause.) Mr. Pierpoint then left the club, and was escorted home by a large crowd,several persons carrying torches. Great excitement prevailed,and it was some hours before it subsided and the streets resumed their usual appear- ance. -- CONGRATULATING THE CANDIDATES.

On Monday night, about an hour alter the declaration of the pall Mr. Scott received the following congrat- ulation on his plucky fight for Liberalism in Warrington "Hearty congratulations from the National Liberal Club on your splendid fight • regret result." The following courteous telegrams were also exchanged between Mr. Scott and Mr. Pierpoint on Tuesday : "To IL Pierpoint, St. Austin's. Warrington.—Not on political grounds, but on friendly, I ask you to accept my best congratulations on your victory yesterday. Kindest regards.—PERCIVAL B. SCOTT." To this message Mr. Pierpoint replied "To P. B. Scott, Manchester.—Very many thanks for your courteous and friendly massage. Of all con- gratulations none has given me such pleasure as yours. With kindest regards.—ROßEßT PIERPOINT." Mr. Pierpoint also received the following congratula- tion from Captain Middleton, chief agent of the Conser- vative party :—" Hearty congratulations on your victory ."

THE WARRINGTON TEACHERS' ASSOCIATION AND THE CANDIDATES. A number of members of the Warrington and District Teachers' Association waited on both Parliamentary candidates early on Monday, and requested their answers to the following questions : I. Are you iu favour of a State-aided superannuation scheme for elementary teachers ? H. Will you support a Bill to secure reasonable security of tenure, and to prevent the dismissal of a teacher save for a reasonable and assigned cause? 111. Are you in favour of an early raising of the present age for juvenile labour ? IV. Are you in favour of removing Article 73 from the Code ? It may be explained that the last question refers to certain disabilities under which non-collegiate certificated teachers labour. Replies in the affirmative to each question were returned by both Mr. Scott and Mr. Pierpoint.

MEETING OF IRISHMEN ALD CATHOLICS.

On Sunday afternoon, a crowded and enthusiastic meeting of Irishmen and Catholics was held in Ohmy's Circus, in support of Mr. Scott. All the local leaders of the Irish and Catholics were present, and the chair was occupied by Mr. T. Kiernan. The CHAIRMAN in his opening speech ridiculed the idea that it was necessary to be born in Warrington in order to boa member. He said that he did not think many there were born in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) That meeting was called to show that the Catholics and Irish people of Warrington were not going to follow the lead of Mr.Pierpoint. (Applause.) He had been speaking in Brick-st. that week, and since then his Tory friends had never left him alone, especially since they had read the report of his speech in the " Examiner " on Saturday. j At that meeting he had said that the word Tory meant a robber. Well, he had looked at the Imperial dictionary and found that he was quite correct. He would read them the meaning of the word Tory from the dictionary. Mr. Kiernan, amid much laughter read the meaning, and said that the Tories had found that their name was a bad name and had accordingly changed it and called themselves Conservatives, and now they were again changing their name to Liberal Unionists. (Applause.) He contended that the name Tory was very applicable to them in Warrington because they had been stealing from the circular sent round by the Catholic Bishops. He, however, appealed to his hearers to support the Liberal party. He denied that it was the duty of the Catholics to support the Tory party on the education question. He hoped that they would on Monday wrench the Warrington representation I rem the domination of the Tory party who had held it so long. (Applause.) Mr. Murmars then addressed the meeting. He appealed to his fellow countrymen to support the Liberal party who were pledged to Home Rule. He said that they could do much for their countrymen in Ireland,and hoped they would do their duty to them. Referring to the education question, he denied that the Catholics would serve their cause by voting for the Tory party. He would remind the Nonconformists that all the Catholics asked for was equality. (Applause.) He appealed to those present to support Mr. Scott on Monday.

Further addresses were given by Councillor Macabe and Mr. Farrally of Manchester, and Mr. J. C. Dalton, and a vote pledging the meeting to use every legitimate means to secure the return of Mr. Scott was passed unanimously.

AT THE LIBERAL CLUB.

SPEECH BY MR. SCOTT.

After the declaration of the poll there was a rush to the Liberal Club, at which place a good number of people had waited for the result. An impromptu meeting was held in the billiard room, at which Councillor William Pierpoint presided. Mr. Scott on making his appearance in the room was received with loud cheers. Councillor WILLIAM PIERPOINT said that he was sorry that their friend, Mr. Scott, had been defeated in the cause of Liberalism. But if he had been defeated in that cause, he was not disgraced—(applause)—and they, as a party, had no very great reason to feel very despond- ent When they recollected that within a period of eight days, Mr. Scott was a perfect stranger to them, and also that they had had against them the Tory squires of the neighbourhood who had done all they could in vehicular and other little arrangements for the Tories, he must say. that Mr. Scott had made an admirable fight —(applause) —and he had every reason to feel proud and thankful to the Liberal workers for the efforts they bad put forth on his behalf. (Applause.) Their opponents had said that they would win by 2,000, but they had since drepped that to 1,000 and 750, but they were now mightily glad to win at all. (Applause.) He declared that Warrington under proper conditions was a thoroughly Liberal town. (Hear, hear.) They must recollect that the polls of Saturday must have had a depressing effect upon War- rington. Mr. Balfour had got at the head of the poll. If he had been ait the bottom, it would have made a difference to the polls all along the line. (Applause.) In cenclusion, he said that they had every reason to be proud of the efforts they had put forward that day, and they were doubly grateful to Mr. Scott. (Loud applause.) Mr. SCOTT, Wh4 was received with prolonged cheer- ing, said that he was proud of Warrington. (Applause.) They had polled nearly 100 over the last election in 1892, and although they had not reduced the maj )rity, still he claimed this as a victory. (Applause.) He was positive of one thing and that was that the verdict on the next occasion would be reversed. (Loud applause.) They had had much to battle with. They had had the church to fight, the brewers to fight, and what was worse, they had had the country's verdict to fight. (Hear, hear.) He would have been proud if Warrington had led the van to different results this week. He was only sorry that they had not had a stronger candidate to fight their battle. (Loud cries of "No, no.") He was not fishing for compliments, but he wanted to say this, that if they were satisfied with him he was ready to fight the battle again. (Loud and continued ap- plause.)Fle wanted to thank the chairman Mr. Pierpoint, the chairmen of the different wards, the secretaries, his election agent, Mr. Clayton, and all the willing workers who had assisted him that day. He also thanked the Mayor who was the returning officer, for the admirable arrangements he had made for the declaring of the poll. He was also proud of Warrington from another point.He was proud to think that there was a friendly feeling—he was going to say almost brotherly love—between him and his opponent, Mr. Pierpoint. He (Mr. Scott) hadthad a gentleman to fight. He had had an honest opponent, one against whom he had net a word to say. In con- clusion he was only sorry that they had not returned him (Mr. Scott) at the head of the poll, but he hoped to win, and he hoped it might be soon. (Loud applause.) Councillor Roamers said that amongst the leaders of the party, there were few who had advocated that the borough should be fought at this election. The result that day had, however, justified their action. (Loud ap- plause). It would have been a crying shame and dis- grace to the Liberal party, if they had allowed the Conservatives to walk over. (Ap- plause.) The result of that poll bad showed clearly to his mind, that with a candidate who was pre- pared to stand by the constituency in the Liberal inter- est, they were very soon bound to win. (Applause.) For their enceuragement,he would remind them of a circum- stance or two. They were defeated three years ago,and since that time nothing inthe way of organisation or ed- ucation had been done in this borough. He was not go- ing to say what the reason of that was, but speaking after 20 ears' electioneering,he was positive that if they had been able to do during the past three years what they ought to have done,they would have placed Mr. Scott at the head of the poll that day. (Applause.) Re never expected they would win that election,but he never said one word to discourage the workersin that direction. He was too old an electioneerer for that. He had never ex- pected in his own mind that they would have polled even as well as they had done. Let him remind them that it was only nine days since Mr. Scott came to that club without knowing a single soul. In spite of the voting, he would say that Me. elnott, was the most popular man in Warrington to-day. (Loud applause.) Their friends the ladies, and their children had also shown their enthusiasm, and that spol:e well for the future of Liberalism in Warrington. (Applause.) They had got now what they had not had ever since 1880. Fie had seen that day, men who had worked in that election and who had never worked since. He welcomed them back into the ranks of the Liberal party. (Applause.) Their opponents had not been able to pick a hole in the Liberal candidate. (Loud applause.) It was all very well to talk of gentle- manly behaviour on the other side. It could not be otherwise (Hear, hear.) He knew that they had sent their emissaries to Manchester a few days ago with the ehjent of finding out anything to the detriment of Mr. Scott, but they failed in their mission. (Loud applause.) They could not find a single point against him. (Ap- plause.) He came before them as a successful man and as a gentleman, and he (Mr. Roberts) was glad to guar- antee to him on behalf of the Liberals of Warrington, that if he would stick to them, they would stick to him. (Loud and continued applause.) That fight had been purely a scratch fight. They had only had eight days to canvass 8.400 people. He was pe•fectly amazed at the result. He had not the remotest idea that it would have come out nearly so well. It was as good as a win. (Applause.) There had been no faddists among them during that fight. All had sunk their fads and peculiarities upon any single subject. (Hear, hear.) He wished to thank especially - their Irish and Catholic friends. (Applause.) He would like to remind them that there were a great many not upon the register which ought to have been. They must know that it was a fact that the present register upon which the election had been fought was two years old. They should rectify that, and they were going to I make the registration work better than it had ever been be- fore. If they were to be successful, they would have to depend upon the help of those who knew the streets bet- ter than their agents could possibly know. He would urge them to take that defeat calmly. He was glad there had been no bad temper thus far, and they would have no bad temper, but would do all they possibly could to reverse the verdict of that day. It would not be long he believed before they had another opportunity. (Ap- Planso.) In the meantime their candidate would have gained upon the affections of the constituency,and when he came before them again to fight their battle, there would he a very different result. (Loud applause.) MT. T. KIERNAN then addressed the meeting. He ex- pressed the opinion that the Irishmen and Catholics of Warrington had done their duty, and he was sure it was no fault of theirs that the result had not been favourable to Mr. Scott. (Applause.) The meeting then terminated.

SPEECHES AT THE CONSERVATIVE CLUB:

A rush was made to the Conservative Club, and the newly elected member was loudly cheered on making his appearance in the Assembly room. He thanked all his workers for what they had done that day. They had again conferred upon him the honour of representing his native town in the Commons of England. (Applause.) And his impression was that that commission would hold good for at least six years. (Applause.) He would like to say that he owed his very heartiest thanks to those who had worked so loyally for him and for the cause he represented. He was sure they would allow him to name two persons as representative men. He had to particularly thank his old friend Mr. Jchn White —(applause)—and his younger, but very old friend, Mr. Henry Greenall. (Renewed applause.) It would be in- vidious to mention other names but he felt his obliga- tion very much indeed, to them as well as to all who had assisted him in this election. He thanked most heartily the Chairmen of Committees, the captains and the can- vassers for what they had done for him during this short election, as well as for what they had done in times gone by. He desired to remind them that this election, of which he was personally very proud, because they had played this ro.me off their own bat—they had not had to introduce a stranger into the constituency. He desired to say in the presence of all, and in the pre- sence of the Unionists of Warrington,how very much he felt the courtesy which had been shown to him during the three years that were gone—not only by them but by his opponents. Thanks were due to their workers, for it was they who had won the victory. He was only the point of the needle ; they were the needle itself. He hoped the tide would continue to go with thern,as it had begun to go with them, and that thr Marquis of Salis- bury, Mr. Arthur Balfour, the Duke of Devonshire, and Mr..Toapph Chamberlain might find that their services to tte Unionist party were very mach appreciated—Cap. plause)—and that they would be put in a position of power from which the country expects much. And he was sure the country would be very little disappointed. He thanked them again for what they had done for him 1 that night, and he could especially remind them that they had elected one of their own sons.(Loud applause.) Mr.JonNWHITE,who was warmly cheered,said he spoke a few words of encouragement at their first meeting,and that night,the night of the election,he had to offer a few words of congratulation. He would add his personal thanks to all who had worked with Mr. Henry Greenall and himself so heartily and so truly.

MEETING OF WORKINGMEN.

A mass meeting of workingmen was held in Ohmy's Circus, on Saturday afternoon to further the candidature of Mr. Scott. There was a. crowded attendance, and the chair was occupied by Mr. James Evans. There were also present, Mr. Scott, Rev. John Yonge, Messrs. A. H. Crosfield. T. Birchall, S.F., Joseph Leicester, Councillor William Pierpoint, Farnsworth, T. B. Malkin, W. Shaw, Wyllie, John S. Green, T. Wallington, Knockton. E. Wright, Adamson, P. Lane, J. H. Fleming, J.P., T. Kier- nan, J, Winstanley, W. Jones, J. Haley, &c. Mr. MALKIN, who occupied the chair at the commence- ment of the meeting, said that their opponents were getting a little wrong in their statements. A local em- ployer had been speaking on the subject of trade, and , had said that the trade of this country was being ruined by foreign competition. He however, forget to mention why this competition was so keen, and never alluded to the heavy royalties which had to be paid upon the iron and coal of this country. (Hear, hear.) If the royal- ties of this country were no heavier than they were in Belgium or Germany, England would not be so severely handicapped in her commerce. (Applause.) The House of Lords was composed of men who gained large for- tunes out of these royalties, and it WAS for the people of England to say whether those lords were to be their rulers. Let them say that they would no longer be ruled by these men who toiled not, neither did thee spin. (Applause.) During the course of his speech, Mr. Malkin gave some interesting details concerning the royalties of this country, compared with Belgium. Mr. FARNSWORTH thought that that meeting was a healthy sign f )r the state of politics in Warrington. They had had a magnificent meeting the previous evening,and that meeting also augured well for the .Liberal cause. (Applause.) Their candidate, Mr. Scott, had made a marked impression on the Warrington people,and he was sure that he would in the end win Warrington for Liberalism. (Applause.) Mr.Farn worth then proceeded to discuss the Liberal programme at length, which he did in a very lucid and convincing manner. Mr.EvAtis said that there was no possible doubt as to what was the Liberal programme, but he had looltEd in vain for the programme of the Tories. What had Mr. Pierpoint done in the past ? (Nothing) If he had done nothing, were they going to let him represent them again, and continue doing nothing ? (No, no.) Mr. Pierpoint had said that if returned he wauld vote for an Employers' Liability Bill. Well, why did he not vote for the last ? (Applause.) His (,Mr.Evans's) confidence in him had gone, because he had sold thorn once, and he would sell them again. (kpplause.) He hoped that they would support Mr. Scott, and do their utmost to put him at the head of the poll. Mr. SCOTT, who received quite an ovation,thanked the meeting for the reception they had given him. That was the last time they would hear him before the polling day. He had therefore felt that it was his duty to come and say a few words to them. (Appl4use.) There had been a poster put out by the Licensed Victualeers' Assoc- iation of Warrington on which was a certain number of questions which had been forwarded to both candidates on the Local Veto question. They gave the answers of Mr. Pierpoint, but thee said that they had not heard from Mr. Scott. He repudhted that entirely. He did no; write to them, but that was not out of discourtesy to them, but because he thought that if he made it public the previous evening, at the meeting, it would be more beneficial to them. The principles which he had put forth at Friday's meeting he would abide by. (Applause.) With regard to the Homo Rule Bill, he would reply in the words of the Leader of the House of Commons, if they wanted Home Rule, veto the power of the House of Lords. (Applause.) If they removed the rwer from the House of Lords, they would get Home .161 and all they required. (Applause.) If they weaned to strengthen that House they would return Mr. eierpoint. He hoped that they would consider the questions which had been put before them,and in the meantime he wnuld wish them au revoir, but not good-bye. (Loud applause.) Mr. JOSEPH LEICESTER afterwards made a vigorous and humorous speech in which he strongly criticised Mr. Pierpoint's address, and the meeting closed with a unan- imous vote of confidence in Mr. Scott.
