GREAT LIBERAL MEETING AT WARRINGTON.

ENTHUSIASTIC PROCEEDINGS.

On rriday night a great Liberal meeting was held in the Circus, Queen-street,in support of the candi- dature of Mr. Scott, the Liberal candidate. The large circus was packed in every part and large numbers could not gain admittance. The proceedings were of an intensely enthusiastic nature, and augured well for Liberal success on Monday. Mr. A. H. Crostield presided, and amongst those on the platform were : Mr. P. B. Scott, Rev. John Yonge, Councillors Pier- point, J.P., H. Roberts, J.P., G. Plumpton, Messrs. Joseph Leicester. C. Jackson Holmes, Z. Armitage, J.P., J. H. Fleming, J.P. E. J. Guest, J.P., T. Hirchall, J.P., J. Horton, J. Mounfield, W.-4. Allen, R. Storey, W. Brough, J. Summersgill, G. Clay, J. W. Hayward, J.P.,J.Gandy, J. Evans, S. B. Stringer, Farnsworth (National Reform Union), Skelland, J. C. Dalton, J. S. Gar- land, J. Harrison, W.Longshaw, A. Lang, D.Tinnion, P. T. Winskill, Wyllie, J. Longland, T. Kiernan, H. Thornton. Councillor ROBERTS being called upon by the Chair- man, said that although their venerable president, Mr. John Rylands, was not able to be with them that night, he would be there on Monday to vote for Mr Scott.—Loud applause.—He also had a tele- gram from Mr. Symonds who was so much engaged in the High Peak division of Derbyshire, which he was fighting on behalf of the Liberals, that he was unable to be presen that night. The CHAIRMAN, who was received with loud applause, said,there is a saying which our old friend Mr. MacMinniea was fond of quoting, that a Chairman's duty is to be seen and not heard. If, therefore, I cut my remarks as short as possible, and make them as general as possiole, it is not because I am afraid of a detailed discussion, or have sbirked that on other occasions. But time is precious and I shall, as soon as possible.call upon gentlemen much more competent than I am. to expound to you Liberal policy and principles. We do not ask you to support them in the abstract. We ask you to support them as applied and because of the way they are practically applied by the leaders of the late administration. Well might Mr. Asquith say he declined to go to the country with anything like an apology. Is not it significant that you have even Mr. Chamberlain's testimony as to the importance of the work the Government have done. It is true he is pitching into us now, but we can make allowance for that. If you have a poor case yourself, it is convenient to blame the other side, and some people are particularly energetic and assiduous in doing so. You know the tale of the Highlander who went to Lon- don and was met by two of his compatriots there. One of them expressed alarm at the very large and long and wide pair of trousers he was wearing. The other said, however," make Your mind easy, converts are always ent:inciastic " We have had an example of that at Latchford. Two of the chief points on which we are being attacked by converts and by others, are,of course, central planks in our platform. One is Home Rule, and the other Local Veto. Now do not be alarmed, gentle- men, I am not going to let you in for any labyrinthian discussion on either of them. I endorse,as we had good evidence, all those who were present endorsed heartily and absolutely, the eloquent reference to Irish Govern- ment which we had last Monday night from Mr. Drummond. He might have said, and I hone on other occasions we shall hear more from him on that subject. I see Mr. Pierpoint said the other day. that if we re- turned the Conservative Government, or returned him, I forget which, we shall hear no more of Home Rule. Well, I would rather not say anything discourteous, for we fully appreeiate the tone of Mr. Pierpoint's general remarks, and I entirely agree with him as to the man- ner and spirit in which the election ought to be and is be- ing fought. But I am hound to say that was a foolish and inaccurate remark. With increasing population and a complexity of modern business which is too great a burden for the shoulders of Parliament, and more than they can attend to, merely as a matter of convenionce that devolution of Irish business which we call Home Rule, a real measure of responsibility for the conduct of their owe affairs, is bound to come in the end. Mr. Pier- point slys also, we are endeavouring to conceal this. Not a bit of it. We have not the slightest reasen or wish to do so. For I submit that whether you regard it from the stand point of English or Irish, Scotch or Welsh in- terests of history expsrience with nationality ; whether you look at it from a local point of view or Imperial point of view,we have proved that it is as much a mat- ter of wisdom and policy as of plain and elementary justice. I should like to say a word on the Local Veto Bill,because political capital is made out of that against us. I looked with interest for Mr. Balfour's speech upon it. As an eminent,honourable,and conscientious States- man of great ability, I take him to be as good a critic and opponent of the matter as we can consult. With the greatest pert of Mr. Balfour's remarks on the agen- cies to which we most look for greater moderation in drinking in the fissure, so far as they go, I entirely agree. But how far did he go. The whole of his critic- ism was based on abolition. With that I have never professed to heirs sympathy. We know that as regards Warrington it is not a policy that is entertained. The question before us now, is, whether if a majority of our people wish to reduce the number of public-houses by one-fourth, they shall have the power to do so. The Liberal party has already professed to give the owners of tied houses three or five years' notice and a section of the party has also advocated compensation to the work- ing publican, which I think should be introduced and which, mind you, would be a vastly different thing to the monstrous scheme of compensation proposed by Mr. Goschen. Gentlemen, we know perfectly well that Warrington, like most other towns, would be all the better if public opinion were in favour of such a reduc- tion by which, of course, we should eliminate the worst class of Houses. Mr. Balfour also knows it, and un- popular as the Bill is said to be in some quarters, he dare not deny this. It Is easier to criticise than to act, and any Government that proposes definite legislation, and any Government that is leaving offiee is very pro- perly open to criticism. But I ask you when has there been a Government which, in the same space of time, has done more useful work. If we have here with us men cf advanced views who are dissatisfied with its re- cord, I would remind them that the power of Govern- ment as of individuals, is limited, It is no use to ex- expect impossibilities. I see Mr. Chamberlain said the other day " If you feel that during the last three years your trade has gone from bad to worse, if you know that confidence has been destroyed, that employment has diminished, that there has been no ameliora- tion n the condition of the masses of the people, then I say you will, sa sensible men, vote for my friend Mr. Stanley, and you will vote in condemnation of the policy which has pro- ' duced these results, and of the men who are responsible for them." We might as well blame Lord Salisbury for the beaming smash. Truly it is lamentable to hear such ' trash from a man of such great powers. If this is the effect of the cushioned ease he has secured within the sacred precints of the upper ten, I do no think we need envy him the social prestige he has asp' red to. This sort of talk always reminds me of a talc that will be familiar to you, but which, I think, bears repeating. The tale of the man who growled at a companion for swindling. The unfortunate individual remonstrated I with him and said it was not fair to blame him for what was his misfortune, .and not hie fault. "Misfortune, I said the other; it is not thee misfortune, it is thee bloomin ignorance as make thee sken." In conclusion, • one word, gentlemen, to those like myself of moderate

views. It is not reasonable to expect or require that we should all of us, on every question, think exactly alike. But I will just say this. We are not a party of social revolution. Our object is to make this more than ever unnecessary and impossible. We shall not pull down the fabric ,if society in order to rig up some brand new jerry-built tenement. Our business as I said this mern- ing, is to adapt our institutions to the growth of the country, so as to secure the greatest good of the greatest number, and we may well ask all those who value, as I value, individual liberty, and the great individualist fabric of society, whether you can have any sure foun- dation, whether you ought to have any other founda- tion for it. I hope we have most of us fully and finally made up our minds on that point,, and believe that the late Government was doing honest and noble work to- wards that end. If there be any here who are yet un- cenvinced I hope they will remain so no longer at the conclusion of our meeting, and I have therefore, with that object, great pleasure in 'calling I,upon.—Loud ap- plause.

Mr. Scorr, who had a splendid receptionvsaid that he had to thank them again for the grand reception they had given the party to which be belonged. He did not claim it as a personal matter but for the party whieh deserved it. He wished to refer to a point which his political friends had tried to make the most they could of. They said he was a carpet bagger. (Laughter.) Whatever else he wag, he was not a carpet bagger. (Loud applause.) If he was a carpet bagger what thee was the Right Hon. Arthur James Balfour. (Loud applause.) What was the Marquis of Lorne.—Hear, hear.—These were two gentlemen who he thought had the right to come to Manchester to contest the Manchester consti- tuencies just ae much as a Manchester man had the right to come and contest it himelf.—Applause.—He did not come there to ask them to have him, but he was asked to come by them.—A Voice : " Give 'em what MacMinnies gave them," and loud applause.—He had come there to fight, and what was more he had come to win—lend applause—and he had come to stay.—Loud applause.—lf they did him the honour of electing him as he believed they would do—a pplause,—if they returned him to Parliament next Monday—we will,—he hoped that as long as health was spared to him, to do such as would justify them supporting him for many years to come.—Loud applause.—Were they going to return him to Parliament—Yes, yes.—Then do him the best turn they could and support him next Monday—Loud applause.—During the course of his election campaign he had not said one word be could not honestly support, and being convinced he said that his age was not a drawback to him. (Loud applause.) With regard to Local Veto he denied that the Bill robbed the poor man of his beer. They could enjoy every license in Warrington if they wished. The Bill would simply Jo this, it would give the power to the ratepayers of saying whether they required one public-house or a dozen. (Hear, hear.) If he were re- turned to Parliament he should be in favour of a Local Veto Bill which shall have a clause inserted doing away with the tied house system. —Loud Applause.—A deputation had that day waited upon him from the Warrington wiredrawers, and they put a number of qnestions to him all of which he was able to answer in the affirma- tive. He was in favour of the proposal that in all great specification a clause should be inserted I requiring that in all great contracts wire of British manufacture shall be used.—Loud and con- tinued applause.—He was against the Government go- ing outside England to place its orders for wnat England needs, and any legislation which tried to give orders to firms not in England would have his most strenuous opposition.—Loud applause.—With regard to the House of Lords he was in favour of no half-hearted work. He did not want to sweep the peer's house away but he would sweep their power of I veto away.—Applause.—lf they returned a Liberal gentleman to power they might be sure that not one of the Bills which were passed in the last House of Com- mons and which would be passed again until they had dealt with this veto of the House of Lords.—Hear, hear. They were told that the House of Lords never stood in the way of the people, but they were a long time in making up their minds, and they, the Liberal party,were going to wait that time again.—Loud applause. Miss GARLAND, who was met with a moat enthusiastic reception, said they had a very great fight before them. They remembered that in 1892 they fought the election on the question of Home Rule for Ireland. (Applause.) This time the election depended on a greater issue, it was the question of the House of Lords. (Loud applause.) They knew that the people of Great Britain were supposed to govern themselves. They sang such songs as "Rule Brittania," "Britons 'never shall be slaves," when they knew perfectly well that they were absolutely the political slaves of the Lords. (Hear,hear.) There was spent something like a million of money at the last general election in order to obtain the feeling and wishes of the peeple of the country. Lard Salisbury appealed to the electors to settle the Irish question and they did settle it.—Cheers. —But what had we, after there had been spent eighty-five days—the greatest num• bet that was ever spent in the British House of Commons on any measure. When it went to the Upper House,Lord Salisbury settled Os question himself.—Hear, hear, and applause.

The Tlev. JOHN Yomok who was well received said that that was the largest political meeting that had been held in Warrington for the past 19 years.—Applause.— Although he knew perfectly well that meetings did not win elections, it was a matter of congratulation that on Friday night, which was voted as the worst possible for meetings, they had such a rnagnificnet gathering a that that night.—Applause.—

At tnis point, some noise was caused by persons throwing at the outside of the build- ing, whereupon Mr. Yonge exclaimed "It appears that the Conservatives of Warrington are already firing the salute of the coming Liberal victory.—Loud laughter and applause.—Continuing, he said he rose to move a resolution pledging the meeting to raise every legitimate means to secure Mr. Scott's return on Monday.—Applause.—He said that he believed that Mr. Scott was the man for Warrington. Much had been made of the fact that Mr. Scott was not Warrington born.—Laughter.—Well he himself had been groaning for 19 years under that terrible calamity—laughter.— but he did not think that that had added to his whitened hair, although it might be white from other causes.—Laughter.—He believed that a very large proportion of the people in Warring- ton were not Warrington born—Applause.— The clergy of the town were not Warrington born, and the Mayor, if he was informed aright, was not Warring- ton born.—Applause.—He hoped that this ridiculous I charge would not be heard again.—Applause.—Mr. Yonge then proceeded to deal with the House of Lords and in conclusion lie appealed to the Nonconformist body of the town. He asked those who had been estranged by Home Rule, were they going to vote for Government which was goin-r again.t all their rater eats in matters of education and religion 2—Applause.—How a Nonconformist could sunoort Toryism was a nut which he could ntst crack.—L pighter and applause.—To temperance men who were Tories, he would also appeal, for what would they get from the Tory party.—Hear, hear and applause. Mr. A. BIRCH seconded the resolution. He said that he was one of those who did not agree with Home Rule 'at the last election, but he was an out and out Liberal. and he did not go against the Liberals. He hoped that Mr. Scott would be returned on Monday. The Liberal Government had been the best for working men. What hal they done with the coal strike, the Leicester strike? Was that not to the advanta,fe of working men. The working men had now the p ever to say what should be done. This is a great responsibility and he hoped they would see that they were on the right side on Monday by returning Mr. Scott at the head the poll.—loud applause.—

Mr. PATRICK LANE supportel the reS ution. He said that ha was not born in Warrtn,gton, but he had a vote and he knew how to record tnat vote on Monday. (Ap- plause.) He had come there that night to call upon his countrymen to go and do their duty on Monday, be- cause thre was not an Irishmm, that was not blinded, who did not know that the Liberal party was the party for which it was his interest to vote. (Loud applause.) The Liberal Government had gone as far as they could go to ive Home Rule to Inland. They had ncw the House of Lords to tacklo, nd then Home Rule would be won. Let them rally round Mr. Scott, and don't tire the canvassers hut come themselves to the poll and come early.—Loud applauso.—Ele denied that the Catholics were at Mr. Pierpoint's back.—Loud ap- plause.

The resolution was then put to the meeting, and was carried unanimously amidst great enthusiasm. Mr. James Moon, Liberal candidate for the Newton Division, said that he had just come from a very enthus- iastic meeting at Newton, and judging from that magni- ficent meeting, he felt that the flowing tide was with the Liberal party.—Loud applause. Mr. Jossert LEICESTER, a native of Warrington, and formerly member for West Htm subsequently addressed the meeting ilan effective pp ,och whicit wa sthoroughly appreciated by the a triience.

PARGATE SCHOOL EXAMINATION REPORT. The report of her Majesty's Inspector of Selools has been received respecting the recent examina- tion of Padgate schools in secular knowledge. It is as follows :—" Mixed school : The children are in excellent ord-,r, bright and interested in their work, which they do very creclitably,with accuracy, intelligence and neatness, notwithstanding the re- cent outbreak of sickness among shem, and the consequent closing of the school for six weeks. The general condition of the school bars strong testi- mony to both sound and intelligent methods of in- struction and discipline. Infants' class : This class is sufficiently disciplined and successfully taught." The report is the hest the school has ever obtained. They had tried both side; of the question, and they knew which was the hest side. There was no good in "boozing." Let them give up the drink and sign the temperance pledge. What would he , have done in his old days if he had kept on drink- ing? Why, he would have ended his days in the Workhouse as many othe:s have done. Mr. Thomas Harrison, of Oldham, also spoke. AN ELECTION ACCIDENT. _ _

On Manday evening Alfred Fitton, 10 years of age, and living at 97, Catherine-st , was admitted into the Infirmary, suffering from a broken arm. It appears that the lad.with others,was starplinz near the Town Halhawaiting, the reeult of the poll,when he slipped over a stone and fell to the ground, re- ceiving the injury named. BOWING CLUB PICNICS.

On Saturday afternoon, the members of the Choetham's Arms Bowling Club. Dunscar, near Bolton, numbering 46, had a picnic to the Horse Shoe Inn, Croft, where they arrived in waggonettes at 3.30 Shortly afterwards they commenced a bowl- ing match an the bowling green(whieh was in good condition) for four prizes. After a good contest .T. Brooks, sen., won the first prize, S. Holt the second, and W. Shepherd the third. The consolation prize was won by R. Brookes. At 530 the party sat down to an exeellent knife and fork tea served by Mr. and Mrs. Mains, the host and hostess. On the same afternoon (Saturday), the members of the bowling club held at the house of Mr. Holland, of the Gardeners' Arms Inn, Harpurhey. near Man- chester, had a picnic to the G-meral Elliot Hotel Croft, and had a bowling handicap on the bowling green, when the sum of £8 was given to be divided into five prizes. The number of competitors was. 35. After the third rGund, owing to therein, the handicap was postponed until Wednesday next, when the final heats will he played or a Cemetery Inn Green, Harpurhey. At two n'clo(dt the party partook of dinner, and a knife and fork tea at 4.313 p.m., latered for by Mr. Henry Walker, the pro- prietor of the hotel. The same afternoon the following picnic parties visited the hotel Alfred Hughes' employes, Salford, numbering 33 Mr. John Haves's employes, Lockstock Junction.. numbering 16; Mr. Barwy's Employes, of Fern- worth, nymbering 40; Mr. Melledey's employes, of Middle Hutton. numbering 33. A densely crowded meeting in upport of the candidature of Sir John T. Brunner, Bart., was held in the Public Hall, Runcorn, on Monday evening. Prior to the commencement of the meeting the audience whiled away the time in singing popular Liberal songs. The chair was occupied by Mr. Philip Speakman, J.P.,C.C.,and he was supported on the platform by Sir John and Lady Brunner, Dr. Bowman, Revs. T. Braman and J. Stafford, Messrs. S. Taylor, J.P.,C.C., T. Handley, J.P., T. Rigby, J.P., A. R. Norman, J. R. Frith, Jos. Bridgewater, ft. W. Pierpoint, M. L. Davies, D. Bisbrown, William Handley, W. 11. Chadwick, E. Gatley,R. Posnett, G. Bellamy, J. L. Frith, and others. On Sir John and Lady Brunner entering the hall, a scene Of unbounded enthusiasm ensued. The CHLIRMAN said it was now over three years since they had the pleasure, as he had, of voting for their member of Parliament, Sir John Brunner. And now the time had come, through Ile fault of their own, for them to have another opportunity of voting for him, an opportunity which be hoped they would all embrace. , (Applause). They all knew that for the last ten years,' since 1885 to 1895, with the exception of one year, Sir John Brunner had faithfully represented them in Parliament. The last three years of Sir John Stunner's life had been years of very great trial, and that trial had been made in the interests of his cluiltry, in their interests, and in the interests of the division, and he was sure that the electors would again return Sir John Brunner at the head of the pull.—Applause.— He—the chairman—had stated at Northwich that he trusted the present contest would be free from person- alities.—Applause.—He was sure there was sufficient in Imperial politics for Sir John Brunner and everybody in the division to talk about in a good tempered way without indulging in personalities. If they stuck to those high Liberal principles which their member had so worthily represented,he felt sure they would succeed, and would b. able to shake hands with their opponents at the close of the contest. He felt sure they would keep harmony, which was most important to enaure success. He advised them to let no one draw a red herring across their path. Their machinery in the division was well-oiled, and it would work well to the end. In conclusion he referred to the Parish Councils Bill passed by the late Government, which, he said,was a great measure of social reform.—Applause.— Mr. A. R. Norman moved the following resolution : " That this meeting heartily approves of thh candida- ture of Sir John Brunner, for Northwich division, and pledges itself to use every legitimate effort to secure his return." He said he had supported similar resolutions, but he candidly confessed that he never so heartily sup- ported it than he did that night. Referring to the defeat of Sir William Harcourt, he said he was very much mis- taken if the Liberals of the country, instead of allowing it to depress them did not throw themselves more heartily into the contest. Sir William Harcourt had fallen a victim to local veto. There were many misre- presentations made about the Bill. The opposite party told them that it meant the taking away of the poor man's beee. But it was simply propose I to transfer the power which the magistrates had to the hands of the public. That surely was a righteous measure, and he believed that a great many of the magistrates would be glad to be rid of the responsibility of granting licenses. Be admired Sir William Harcourt for the way he had stuck to that measure. He had done so, knowing that he had some of the largest interest in drink in his own constituency. But they must remem- ber that they could not get everything at once. They would have to he fought for again and again and if they were right and just they must obtain them in the end. Alluding to the work of the late Government he said they had stuck to their guns and had done their very utmost to carry out the pledges they gave to the coun- try. He was glad that the question of the Meuse of Lords was tieing brought to the front That question would have to be dealt with very strictly and very quickly. He had the deepest sympathy for a man like Lard Rosebery who had to spend his time in the House of Lords when he knew perfectly well that he might RS well whistle to the wind as speak to the lords. The speaker then referred to the great Budget Bill of 1894. which he said was a beneficent act. Speaking about the trade of the country he said it Was the greatest fal- lacy to talk about it being good when the Tories were in power, and bad when the Liberals were. He did not think Parliament affected the trade of the country. If there is anything in it at all he should say that the Liberals had it because they tried to mind their own business ; tried to meet the wants of the people ; and by preserving peace, by acting on laws of righteousness, laws of right and not might, which tended to increase trade if anything did. He thought they all knew the reason of had trade better than that. One of the chief reasons was the opposition we received in foreign coun- tries. both in Germany and America. Be could also point out that while they had had trade very had in this country it had been worse in the coloniee. They must remember that trade had been bad all the world over, and as we were the great producingcoontry of the world we had suffered in consequence. In conclusion,he urged the electors to put Sir John Brunner at the head of the poll. (Applause.) Mr. Gatley in seconding the resolution said Sir John Brunner was the best man they could have to represent that division. He (Mr. Gatley) had always found him approachable when matters concerning the working classes had to he laid before them. He might say that in the House of Commons and cut of it, the watermen which he (the speaker) represented, and the working- men in general,the trades' unionists, whom he was proud to represent, had a greet deal to thank Sir John Brunner for. They had to thank him that the watermen were able to voice their grievances on the Royal Commission of Labour. (Anplause). He might also say that Sir John Brunner had given him a pledge that he was pre- pared to stand by the Employers' Liability Act without contracting out. (Loud applause.) Why did they not want contracting out? Let him give them two facts. A short time ago a man got killed ih Liverpool. He Was employed by a railway company who enforced him to , contract out. He left a wife and nine children and they were compensated to the extent of £BO. Very shortly afterwards another man employed as a carter Was killed, and he had not contracted out of the Employers' Liability Act. The society of which he was a member took up the case, and they succeeded in getting £750 as compensation. (Applause.) He thought that was quite sufficient to educate working- ' men to the fact that they must stand by the Employers' ILiability Act as a whole, and not in the cnetracting out clause. He knew that Sir John Brunner had the cause of the workingmen at heart. He was not within giving them 'a Pledge as !to what he was prepared to do in the future. He (Mr. Gatley) laid before him a programme as laid down at a conference of the branches at Manchester on the 11th inst., and be was proud to be able to inform his fellow trades' unionists that Sir John Brunner was pre- pared to stand by it with the exceptic n of three matters, and he (Mr. Gatley) could tell them that he was not pre- pared to stand by those three himself. (Laughter and applause.) He liked justice, and he thought the work- ingman should obtain the just reward of his labour. (Applause.) In Sir John, they had a man that. would not countenance tyranny and oppression. He saw. that on the other platform there was arrainged against them some of the greatest oppressors that had been in his (Mr. Gatlev's) time. He thought it would be a disadvantage to be without a second chamber, hut he did think that the prerogative of that house ought to be curtailed so that when the Commons had given its voice on a measure that house should not have the power to say nay to it. (Loud applause.) He also alluded to the Employers' Liability Bill, and the Municipal Franchise Bill, which the Lords had destroyed. Whenever a poor fellow got hurt or killed in his employment at their works they had never waited to settle the question as to who was in fault, but they had always given the compensation. THE CANDIDATES AND LABOUR QUESTIONS. The followieg questions which were adopted by the Parliamentary Committee of the Trades Union Congress at Manchester on the 10th inst. have been forwarded by Mr. Gatley, of Ruccorn, to Sir John Brunner and Mr. Thomas Ward. The questions were :—L The amend- ment of the Jury Law so as to pay jurymen for their services,and make it eligible for all Parliamentary voters to become jurymen and to abolish the present system of calling Grand Juries ; (2) to prevent the landing of pauper aliens; (3) the amendments to Mines Act so as to secure the objects desired by the miners' organisations of the country ; (4) accommodation for seamen so as to give an adequate number of cubic feet for breathing space ; (5) amendments to allotments act on the lines of previous congress resolutions ; (6) employers' liability bill ; (7) general eight hours' day applying to mines, bakehouses, fac- tories, and other workshops ; (8) Government contracts so as to include Trade Union rate of wages, Trade Union conditions of labour and the abolition of all contracts entered into; (9) law of conspiracy so as to repeal the law which makes picketing illegal; (10) manning of ships so as to make it impossible for ships to be under manned ; (11) nationalisation of land, the means of production, distribution and exchanges ; (12) old age pensions; (13) payment of members, and returning officers' fees ; 1,14) reform of Parliamentary procedure with a view to pre- vent the waste of Parliamentary time ; (15) registration reform; (16) reform of the poor law system ; (17) more adequate inspection in mines, factories, railways, docks, and worashops ; (la) the truck act to prevent contract- ing out and to give it a general application; (19) the total abolition of the House of Lords ; (20) the reform of the law of libel.

Sir John Brunner's reply is favourable to nearly all the questions. Referring to the 4th he states that common humanity states that it should be obtained for them. Concerning the sth, he is grateful that the frades Union Congress should take up the case. With regard to the Employers' Liability Pill, he states that every voter in the constituency knows that tie was against contracting out. The firm of which he was head paid compensatism in all cases of death from permanent injury without considering whether it was the man's own fault or not. As to the general eight hours bill the firm adopted it six years ago, and as a consequence the men were healthier, steadier, and happier and delighted with the result. With reference to Government contracts, the late Government had done splendid work in carrying out the orders of the House of Commons to pay Union wages and he supported them. The law of conspiracy was in many respects out of date and required amend- ing. Concerning the undermanning of ships,theri quest was reasonable not only to seamen but to society at large. He considered question elf-ven to be the wildest possible nonsense. With regard to question 12, he was strongly in favour of it,but no practical scheme had yet been proposed. He approved of payment of members, and as regarded the reform of Parliamentary procedure his views were proverbially known. He was in favour of question 17 and as to 18, better inspection would be car- ried out under Mr. Asquith's Act which the Tories feught bitterly and whien the House of Lords dared not thrJw out. Questions 18 and 20 he was in favour of. Regarding 19 he did not wish to abolish the House of Lords no more than he wished to abolish the Crown. But the veto of the Crown had gork long ago, and he wished to abolish the veto of the House of Lords.

Mr. Ward replied that it was impossible for anyone to express any definite opinion upon such a multi- tude of subk•cts as those contained iu the pro- gramme. If the framers of such a programme would consider that a candidate for Parliament for any district should represent not anyone section of that district, however numerous the electors might be, but as far as consistent with the general good the interests of all sections, there would be no attempt to exact pledges on so many questions. No member of Parliament would be better than a machine who fettered himself with pledges of the kind they desired. The principle which guided him was justice to all parties and special privileges to none. Whilst agreeing with several of the items of the programme and willing at all times to fully consider and thoroughly discuss any matter bearing upon the interests of labour generally he was not prepared to give any assent whatever to several other parts of the programme. He did not see the justice of dealing unfairly by part of the community. If labour representatives would follow out the same measure of justice to those who would suffer by the carrying out of their programme as they asked for themselves, there would be greater probability of good resulting to all. lithe legislation of this country was ever forced into the making of special laws for powerful selfish interests of certain Sections of the com- munity at large, and without having any consideration whatever to the injury and injustice done to some, a state of affairs would be introduced disadvantageous to everybody. He would he fully prepared to consider any question bearing upon lahmr.and,as tar as he could, en- deavour to promote anything brought forward in the in- terests of labour that would neither injure the trade of the community nor the general welfare.

MEETING AT WESTON.

A well-attended open-air meeting was held at the Crosses, Weston, on Monday evening. Mr. Thomas Handley, J.P., occupied the chair and stated that they were met that night to support the candidature of their old friend,Sir John Brunner. He had served them well, and had taken a great interest in everything that per- tained to the district, not only politically but education- I ally. (Applause.) Sir John Brunner who met with a hearty. reception, said he was very glad to have the opportunity of addressing the men of Weston,who were now old friends of his. (Applause.) He did not want them to send him to another constituency becalm he told them frankly,he had not a heart large enough for another. (Applause.) After referring to the postponement of the opening of the Runcorn Technical Institute, he said the Tories were telling them everywhere that trade was had because of the Liberal Government who had been in rower for the last three years. Upon this meter he wished to dwell for a few minutes. The year 1890 was the hest year for trade that had ever been known in the history of the country. But at that time there came a great disaeterupon the mercantile world, and hp that he did not mesa the mercantile world of England alone, but the ,whole world. That was the failure of the great house of Baring Bros,—a great banking house I which hal relations with the whole world. Their busi- ness was the negotiation of loans with foreign countries. England had within the last 50 or 60 years lent a great deal of money to foreign countries. When we issued this money, trade was good, becalia- instead of lending the mores' to these countries we sent grinds. Well, this larg eh'-use of Baring's failed at the end of the year 1889 lowing to their lending money inindiciously to countries 'that could not pay interest. They lent millions of money to Ihe South American republics which was a most risky, proceeding. For instance they spent over a million of money in providing a large water works in the Argen- tine republic, and when they were finished, the people found that the water WAS so dear that they could not afford to take it, and the whole thing came to ruin, The Argentine republic failed to pay their debts and at the same time other reDuhliee in South America failed to pay also. This had a result on the Australian banks which fell one after another until they were on the verge of ruin. All this occurred during the time of the ('onservative Government, frem 189') to 1892, and the imports and exports had fallen £36,000,000 sinze then the fall had amounted to another £l2OOOOOO. They would see that three quarters of the drop occurred dur- ing the time of the Tory Government. and only one fourth during the time of the Liberal Government. It. was quite clear, therefore, that the Liberal Government Was not the cause of had trade. Sneaking with reeerd to the regulation and enntrol nf the liquor traffic he said the author of the Bill, Sir William Harcourt, had lost his seat at Derby. That defeat he thought was the result of a great indiscretion wluch he was snrry to say certain misguided individuals in the town of Darby had been guilty of. LIBERAL MEETING AT HALTON.

There 17;;;Ti-aTge-gTt-heringe at the -O-p-e-t-t- air meeting at Elalton on Tuesday evening in support of the candida- ture of Sir John Brunner. Mr. Thomas Handley, J.P., conducted the meetink , and amongst those present were Sir John and Lady Brunner, Revs. J. Simpson, J. Stafford, Messrs. Williams (National Liberal Club), Looker, Barrow, E. Gatley, G. Bellamy, and others. A resolution pledv,ing the meeting to use every legiti- mate means to secure Sir John Brunner's return was I moved by Mr. Looker, seconded by Mr. Barrow. The latter said a great cry was being made by the Conserva- tives about bad trade. He contended that neither Lord Rosebery nor Lord Salisbury could affect the trade of the country. Let him give them an instance. Five years since, bleaching powder was selling at the Liver- pool chemical market at £4 73. 63. per ton. To-day the price was £7. It was impossible for any Government to alter the price which was now being charged. They could not force the owners to sell at a cheaper rate. Mr. Jephoon supported the resolution, and referred to Sir John Brunner'a generosity during the recent distress. He also spoke ot the necessity of the Disestablishment of the Church, and also made reference to the Local Veto Bill. He denied that the Bill would rob the poor man of his beer, and said he was afraid it would be a long time before they would be able to get a two-thirds majority to close a public-house at Halton. Mr. Williams of the National Liberal Club, London, then addressed the meeting at great length. esaid the cry about the Liberals being responsible for bad trade was one of the greatest pieces of humbug that had aver been raised. (Applause.) It was being done to hood- wink the electors. No Government was directly res- ponsible for the fluctuations in our trade. In alluding to Mr. Ward's address he referred to the arrogance of the Tory party in claiming to be the only party who were en leavouring to make the country great. He proceeded to criticise several remarks in Mr. Ward's address, and referred to the beneficent legislation of the late Liberal Government. In conclusion he alluded to the House of Lords, and asked them to support Sir John Brunner. ,1 Sir John Brunner, who met with a hearty reception, I dealt in the first place, with the veto of the House of Lords which, he said, should be abolished. Proceeding to speak of the Unionist cry that the Liberal Govern- ment had caused bad trade, he wished to know whether they could blame the Tory Government for the rain which was falling just now? (A Voice: "Yes" and loud laughter.) He (Mr. Brunner thought the man who was of that opinion should be put in the Liverpool Museum. (Laughter.) He affirmed that rash speculation had a great deal to do with the bad trade which the country had suffered from recently. It was greatly due to the failure of the great house of Baring •Brothers who had been speculating heavily in the Republic of Argentina. Surely the Liberal Government coald not be blamed for that, and when anyone told them that the Liberals had , caused bad trade he hoped they would give them their answer. In conclusion he spoke of the Local Vote Bill and said no one had a better right to say how many r licensed houses they would have in their midst than the people themselves. He asked for their support on the 24th inst., and said their interests would always be supreme with him. Whenever their interests clashed with his, his should go down and theirs should go up. (Loud applause.) The resolution was then put to the meeting and carried unanimously.

QUESTIONS BY SCHOOL TEACHERS.

On behalf of the Runcorn Teachers' Association the Secretary has pot the following questions to the Parlia- mentary candidates of the Northwich Division :-1. Will you vote for immediate effect being given to the recommendations of the Education Commission, 1888 ? 2. Will you vote for the insertion to the Education Code of a memorandum of agreement between teachers and managers, such as would prevent the dismissal of the teachers except for a reasonable and assigned cause? 3. Will you vote for such representation of the inter- ests of public elementary schools upon any public bodies charged with the control of the higher education effort, organised or assisted by the State or by local a uthorities as shall secure to the most promising of the elementary school pupils full opportunity for the enjoyments of all educational facilities now provided or added and here- after to he provided or added from public sources ? Will you vote for the early raising of the present age for juvenile labour of any kind either in factory, mines, or education acts ?

Both candidates have answered the questions in the affirmative. Sir John Brunner adis " The attainment of your ends will he most beneficial, not alone to your association but to society at largo. I note with special admiration the wise and far seeing aim embodied in your three questions, for as John Knox stated ' Every scholar is an addition to the wealth of the State.' "

TORY MEETING AT FIALTON.

A meeting in support of the candidature of Mr. Ward, the Tory candidate for Northwich,was held at Halton on Friday evening in the Halton schoolroom. Sir Richard M. Brooke, Bart , presided, and there were al.° peesent : Rev. G. D. Wray (vicar), Mr. T. Ward (candidate), Messrs. E. Imison, J.P., H. Reynolds, J. Meadowcroft, J. Stokes, T. Wright, J. E. Jeacock, and others. The following resolution was passed on the motion of Mr. J. W. Lightburn, seconded by Mr. Mesdowcroft, " That this meeting expresses its confidence in Lord Salisbury's Government, and trusts it may be supported by an overwhelming majority at the present election." (Ap- plause.) Mr. Ward then addressed the meeting and re- , viewed the action of the late Government during the past three years which he described as being of a "log rolling" character. Instead of wasting time pulling down churches, disintegrating the Empire, and abolishing the House of Lords, the new Government would extend trade, find work, elevate the condition of the workingman, and find him something to make his old age comfortable. He endorsed that policy, and he asked them to give him the opportunity of suonort,ng it. (Applause.)—The Rev. G. D. Wray proposed a re- solution that Mr. Ward was a fit and prcper person to represent the Northwich Division in Parliament.—Mr. Birch seconded the propocition, which was carried.— vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the meeting.

UNIONIST MEETING AT RUNCORN.

In the Public Hall, Runcorn, OM Wednesday evening,a meeting was held in support of the Unionist candidate, Mr. Thomas Ward. Mr. Charles Reynolds, J.P., occu- pied the chair, and there were present on the platform, the candidate, the Hon. Alan De Tatton Egerton, M.P., Sir Richard Brooke, Bart., Mr. .T. Lowry, Mr.F. Smith, 8.A., Rev. Dr. Preston, Rev. W. H. Stables. Dr. Mc- Kenzie. Messrs. J. Oaklreton, J. J. Meadowcroft. F. A. Lake, J. Ashton,J. J. Speakman, C. L. Evans, r. Tim- mins, J. Timmins, C. Linaker, J. The Chairman said first of all he wished to make apologies for the absence of Mr. John Brundrit, Mr. Henry Reynolds, Mr. Charles Hazlehurst, and Mr. F. R. Norman, who regretted being unable to be present and to help forward the good cause. Referring to Sir John Brunner's generosity during the recent distress, he said he did not think that should be made a ground for supporting him at that election, and they must also be aware that the Company which he repre- sented had to a large extent knocked the bottom out of the trade of Runcorn. It was far from his wish to be personal and he hoped no one would take it as a per- sonal r-fiction.

Mr. Hornby L"wis in speaking of Sir John Brunner's addross referred to the credit that gentleman took with regard to the suh,idence question. But the question had been deferred for a couple of years because North- wieh wanted Winsford to pay fol•tha damage which was chiefly caused by Messrs. Brunner, Mond, end Co, at Northwich. Sir John Biunner also said a great deal of what he had done to reform the Weaver trust. It seemed to him (the speaker) that the greatest henefitters of that would he the firm of Brunner Mend and Co. Whereas ' salt paid something like a shilling a ton, alkali was at a much reduced rate—vis , 81. He • thought that while credit was taken for these two things, there was a great deal to be said on the other side. The alkali works at Runcorn which used to employ six or seven hundred men had now no more than three hundred which was owing simply to the competition of Messrs. Brunner, Mond, and Co. Be afterwards alluded to the Rome Rule Bill, Welsh Disestablishment, and the Local vwn Bill and in conclusion asked them to support Mr. Ward who would brine, hack trade to them and put money in their pockets. (A ppla use.) Mr. Thomas Ward who received a hearty reception said he did not Wand there with the intention of s+i-ifig, any hard words against any man. His friend, Sir John Brunner, was a generous man, and he had not a word to say against his generosity. (Applause.) THE WIDNES ELECTION.

MR. DEACON Al' DITTON.

In continuation of his political tour to the various stations in the Widnes Division, Mr. Deacon addressed a large and enthusiastic open-air meeting at Ditton on Monday evening. Mr. W. Knowles presided,and he was supported by Messrs. H. W. Deacon, H. B. Shores (agent) and Ross-Browe, Liverpool. The Chairman introduced Mr. Deacon as a local man, and one who was ever ready and willing to assist a good cause. Above all, he was a Liberal. (Loud applause.) Mr. H. Wade Deacon, who was loudly applauded, said that if he were returned to the House of Commons he hoped be would use his speaking powers, and not be the same as some gentlemen were. (Loud applause and laughter.) Referring to Mr. Gilliat's utterances. the I speaker said that gentleman had said that the Liberals tried to create schism between employers and employed. Such a statement as that was utterly absurd. What , the Liberals had tried to do was to heal the I wounds that occasionally broke out between masters and men, but the Tories tried to put difficulties in the way of healing them. (Applause.) He made a brief allusion to the coal strike of 1893,and asked which was the party that terminated the affair ? It was L.)rd Rosebery. (Loud and continued applause.) "fhat was, he believed,the first time that a Prime Minis- ter had interfered in any labour dispute, and it was to the credit of the Liberal party that of their own choos- ing they had made so successful an enterprise. (Cheers.) There was tbe great strike among the cab men in Lou- don, and,although it did not immediately concern them, it was well worth remembering that the late Govern- ment were the persons who settled the affair, and with success too. Mr. Deacon made a reference to the boot and shoe dispute at Leicester, and claimed for the Liberal party the satistaction of interfering with success. Now, he contended that anyone who ItEnw the Liberal party would not think .of presuming that they tried to sow discord between masters and men. His point was that they should try to keep trade in their country, and if by disputes other countries got the trade, then they must inevitably suffer. (Hear, hear.) Ween they got trade the great thing they had to do was to see that the profit was Equitably shared between employers and em- ployed. (Loud cheers.) The Tory party were very Rind of talking about 'natters of conciliation, but they

repeatedly blocked the Conciliation Bill of the late Government, with the result that it had to be abandoned in September, there being no time to proceed with it. He asked them, after these facts, whether the Tory party were the party to trust with their affairs. (" No, no.") The Government had formulated a Bill—the Employers' Liability Bill—and this the Government had had to drop through the interference of the House of , Lords, who would insert a clause allowing men to "con- tract out." The Factory Act was due to Mr. Asquith, and it had been passed by the House of Lords. Ile gave this as an instance of the way in which tha Lords treated Bills. When the Tories were in power, or there was the probability of their coming into power, there was no difficulty in passing Bills. But when the Liberals were in power it was absolutely impossible to bring measures to any satisfactory result. Mr. Gilliat said that if a Parliament were set up in Dublin it would be independent. That was absolutely absurd. It never was proposed to give Ireland an independent Parliament; what it was proposed to do was to grant Home Rule to Ireland, which was the same as giving Lancashire a measure like the County Council. The Tories ridiculed them with being dig-Unionists, but the fact was they (the Liberals) were Unionists in the true sense of the word. (Cheers.) Ireland had never dreamed of raising and supporting an army or a navy. Ireland never wished to be separate. They all wished to live under the same flag, and he assured them that Ireland would always be ready and willing to fight side by side when Home Rule was granted, as she had done in the past with the soldiers t f this country. (Applause.) Ireland had been growing more peaceable year by year under Liberal rule, and it was by granting this measure of Home Rule that they hoped to avoid trouble, and so in the future ensure peace tor that coun- try. Mr. Gilliat told them that they had lost America, but he (Mr. Gilliat) reminded them that America was lost by not granting Home Rule. (Loud applause.) Mr. Gilliat excused a good deal, and said that they (the Tories) gave a deal of thought to labour and social questions. Well, he thought that he had shown them the attention and thought that the Tories paid to ques- tions of such importance. Concluding, Mr. Deacon al- luded to the question of agriculture, and observed that the Tory party had bitterly opposed Mr. Lambert's Land Bill, the provisions of which he explained. It , seemed to him that the great object of the Tory party was to support the landlord party, and neglect tin far- mers and labsurers. (Applause.)

A vote of thanks, s proposed by the Chairman, and seconded by Mr. Ross-Brown, was carried unanimously. Mr. Deacon subsequently drove to St. Michael's Schcols, where he addressed a crowded meeting, the room being full to its utmost capacity. The chair was occupied by Mr. James Brown. In the course of his observations, Mr. Deacon refuted the statement that Governments were responsible for the trade of a country, and that the Tories gave better trade, and in support of his contention read sta- tistics showing that under the Tory Govern- ment the export trade had decreased 40 millions, and the import 33 millions. (Applause.) He further dealt with the labour questions of the day, and claimed that it was the Liberal party that were the pioneers of all progressive reform. Asked whether he would upport a Bill to remove the injustice of a man being deprived of his vote owing to accepting temporary relief through want of employment, Mr. Deacon said he would give any such Bill his cordial approval. (Cheers.) A vote of confidence in Mr. Deacon was un- animously passed.

Pownsll conducted the choir. The organ was presided at by Mr. A. Tim- mins, jun. The collections realised over £lO. SON 3 OF TEMP 2 ERANCE —HALF-YEARLY MEETING. —..• . . .

This order held its half-yearly meeting in the S.a.ris of Temperance Hall, Weston, on Monday evening Repre- sentatives were present from all parts of the Cheshire grand division, including Northwich, Runcorn, Weston, Crewe, Chester, and Winsford. The Runcorn represen- tatives and officers consisted of the following :—Messrs. Thomas J. Holt, Thomas Clarke, Daniel Bisbrowne, Richard Key, Thomas Heesom, James Johnson, Thomas Whyte, David Smith, John Jones, Samuel Henderson and Samuel M. Temperley. Mr. William Heath,G.W.P., of Crewe, occupied the chair. The secretary, Mr. S.M. Temperley. submitted the numerica! and financial report for the past half-year, and the latter showed an in- crease in the funds. They were adopted. The repre- sentatives present then considered the new rules at con- siderable length, and ultimately they were deemed satis- factory and passed. Mr.Temperley resigned his position as grand scribe in consequence of leaving the district. Tie was heartily thanked for his services and Brothers Hughes (Chester)and Edward Key (Runcorn) nominated. The voting resulted in the election of the latter. It was resolved that the next meeting of the Grand Division should be held at Chester at the beginning of next year. Votes of thanks to officers closed the gathering. ANOTHER LAD SENT TO A REFORMATORY. Before Messrs. T. Handley and E. Imison at Runcorn Police Court, on Wednesday morning, a lad, 14 years of 'age, named Martin Gilligan, of Cooper-st., Riincorn,was charged with stealing a silver watch value £3, and two pennies, the property of Charles Hind, coachman to the Rev. Canon Wood. The prosecutor in his evidence stated that on Monday about noon he placed his watch in the kitchen together with two pennies. He left them there for a short time, but on his return he discovered that the watch and money had been taken. From what the servants told him he gave information to the police. On the previous night (Tuesday) about nine o'clock, he met the prisoner and his father in Greenway-road, and they told him that the watch had been taken back.— P.C. Gregory stated that he charged the prisoner with the theft. He at first admitted that he had spent 19.21., but afterwards denied this, and stated that he found the watch in the Vicarage garden.—The prisoner in reply to the magistrates repeated his statement as to finding the watch.—Martin Gilligan,father of the lad, asked the magistrates to discharge him and promised to send him away from Runcorn from his had companions. The lad had:taken to reading a great many " penny dreadfuls," detective stories, &c. On finding the watch in his pos- session he (witness) immediately returned it to the owner.—lnspector Hicks gave the lad a bad character. The magistrates were of opinion that the lad was being encouraged by someone, and committed him to prison for 14 days,to be followed by confinement in a reforma- ory for Lour years.

Nxw TEMPERANCE HALL—A new building for the order of the Sons of Temperance was opened on Friday, ano that the temperance friends have been in earnest about it may be judged from the fact that the work of erecting it has beeia done by the temperance people themselves. It will provide accommodation for about 100 persons, and will be used as a meeting house and for practices by the drum and fife bawd. The ceremony of opening the building was performed by Master William A. Guest. Two binds, the church brass band, and the Wesleyan brass tand were in attendance. --BEFORE VIE MAGISTRATES.

DISEASED FISH PROSECUTIONS. Yesterday, at the Warrington Borough Police Court, before Mr. S. Chandlev and Alderman Harrison, Joseph Lever, 25, Bishop-st., Cheetham Hill, and fish dealer in the Warrington Market,was summoned for having on the 2nd inst., 202 herrings deposited in the cellar of the Market Hall, which were unfir for food.—Mr. A Browne prosecuted, and said that on Tuesday Inspector Norman visited the cellars ard found four boxes of kippered her- rings wet and decayed. They were taken before Mr. J. W. Hayward, J.P., after being certified to be unfit for food by the deputy Medical Officer of Health, and the former ordered their destruction.—Inspector Norman corroborated, and said that he visited the cellars as the result of a complaint.—Dr.J.G. Gornall deposed that the fish were wet and smelling putrid.—Mr.H.Peet,solicitor, who defended, said that the fish were not intended for sale, and had gone bad between the Saturday evening and the day they were seized, owing to the hot weather. —A fine of 20s. and costs was imposed (22 2. di.), the magistrates remarking that defendant should have made arrangements to prevent the fish going bad in his pos-

-session. William Lowe, 149, Church-st., was charged with having fish in his possession, viz., 921 b., on the 4th July and William Bottomley, 3, Naylor-at., was charged with being the owner of the fish.—Mr. Browne prosecuted, and said that Inspector Norman on the day in questicm saw Lowe with a handcart containing the fish run up Eaydock-st. on catching sight of him. Norman followed and found a quantity of mackerel and flukes decom- posed and unfit for human food. These were likewise examined and destroved.—Lowe denied running away, and said that he was unused to fish and was unaware of their condition. Bottomley, who said nothing,was fined £2 and costs and Lowe ss. and costs. A BRUTAL AsSAULT.—THE " COMEHEDGE CRUSH." At the Warrington Borough Police Court yesterday, William Fleming, 5 Brick-St., and John Morrin, 29 James'-st., were charged with assaulting and beating Patrick McDonnell, 75 Sharp-St., on the 28th June.— McDonnell, the complainant, stated that on Walking Day he was in Gorsey-lane and was going in the swing-boats with his sister when one of a crowd of young men pushed him and he requested them to mind where they were pushing. They then wanted him to fight but he refused. Someone told bins it was the " Cockhedge Crush" and he was turning to go when Morrin struck him in the eye, and the other two rushed upon him and both kicked him while on the floor. F,eming was one who hit him.—A friend of complainant's. Browne, gave corroborative evidence and also said that he received a blow that was aimed at complainant's sister and which laid him up for three days.—Miss McDonnell and a friend also gave evi- dence showing that Norrin struck McDonnell a cowardly blow in the eye, and also kicked him -while on the floor. , Fleming was not so rough.—P.O. Bell said he had to eject the defendants from the field and observed that complainant was partly unconscious for 20 minutes.— A man named Lowe was called for the defence but did not prove anything material.—Morrin was fined 103. and costs and Fleming 5 and costs. STRANGE CASE OF ASSAULT ON THE POLICE. At the Warrington 8-Tough Police Court on Monday before the Mayor and Mr. F. Monks, James Slater, was charged with being drunk and disorderly on Saturday afternoon, and also with assaulting P.C. John Berry at the same time and place.—P.C. Berry stated that at the time stated in the charge he was coming down Church- st., when hornet defendant and his wife togother. The former asked Berry to "lock this woman up." Witness asked him if she was his wife and defendant said "Yes." Witness then asked the woman her name and she replied " Jane Slater," and in answer to a further query as to bow long she had been married, she said "That is enough for you," and prisoner then joined in and struck witness on the tensple. They closed and after a struv.gle witness conveyed defendant to the Police Station —Mrs. Slater gave corroborative evidence ane stated that when she gave her name the constable told her that it was was not her proper name. Further evidence having been given Mr. A. Browne, who appeared for the defence, said that defendant was a bricksetter's labourer and that there was a Thomas Slater, brick- setter, and the police had been asked to look nut for a woman supposed to be living with Thomas Slater. This was the cause of the questioning and Mr. Browne ad- mitted that Slater had had drink,but that he only struck at the officer and did not hit him.—A fine of 51. and costa was imposed for the assault the other charge being dismissed. CHILD NEGLECT—PARENTS SENT TO GAOL. On Tuesday at the Boroush Police Court, Warring- ton, Edward Scully, and Mary Ann Scully. 3, Glass House-yard, Scotland-road, were charged with wilfully neglecting their three children on Jule 13-h and divers Other dates.—l9spector McAteer. N S.P.C.C., said that at 1 30 on the afternoon of the 13th July. he found the male prisoner drunk. Detective Sergeant Monaghan brought him to the police station and he was remanded on Monday until Tuesday. The three children in the house were insufficiently clothecl, the little boy being in a filthy condition. Prisoners did not belong to Warring- ton but travelled about from town to town,and had only been in Warrington about five weeks. They were believed to be the most drunken characters yet before the bench. There was hardly any food in the heuse at the time,and witness was told that the male prisoner sold the furni- ture.—P.C. Amson gave evidence as to also having visited the house on the 13. h July, stating that he found the children crying and no one else in the house. The children were alive with vermin, and when he after- wards saw the female prisoner she had two black eyes which were attributed to her.busband.—Mr. Diley,Mrs. Ward, and Mrs. Davies, neighbours, all stated that they hardly ever saw the woman sober.—Mr. H. Taylor (P.C.M.), in supporting the evidence from a visit he had made said that whest he gave the children some food, they ate ravenously—Both prisoners were sent to gaol for one month's hard laboue each, and the children were ordered to be conveyed to the Workhouse.

DIRECTORS' REPORT.

An extraordinary general meeting of this Campany will be held at the Bank premises,Winwick-st. Warring- ton on Thursday next. The following is a copy of the directors' report which will he then submitted :—The Directors, in submitting to the shareholders the balance sheet of the bank at 30th June, 1895, certified by the auditors, have to report that the position and prospects Of the business continue to be in every way satisfactory. Including £35.031 9=. 3d brought forward at 31st December, 1894, the net balance of profits, after prrvi l- ing ample for all bad and doubtful debts, and rebating the current bills at 5 per cent., is £152,109 3a. 51. From this sum the directors recommend the payment of a dividend at the rate of 19 per cent, per annum, free of income tax, for the quarter ending 30th June. 1895, to be payable on Ist August, 1895, £47,500; dividend at the same rate for the quarter ending 30 h September, 1895. to be payable on the Ist November, 1895, to the shareholders whose names shall be on the register on the 17th October,£47,soo; to bank premises account42.soo; leaving balance to be carried to next account. £34,609 3s. sd.

THE MAN WHO ATE SUPPER.

Mr. Dalton, of Orgarth Hill, Tathwell, Lincs., is a tall, erect man, apparently about 50, with an intelligent looking forehead, honest kindly eyes,and a face suffused with health.

On being interviewed by a newspaper man, Mr. Dalton related with frankness and freedom a very inter- esting experience. " Three years ago last Christmas," he said, " I began to feel ill. I had a pain at my stomach ; I had no appetite, and when I did eat any- thing, wind would come off my stomach until I would be perfectly sick. The pain was very great, in fact I was in agonies ; it would come on at midnight some- times and continue for hours ; I could neither lie nor stand still ; I kept breaking off work through the illness and lost half a year in this way. I consulted one doctor after another, some calling my complaint indigestion, and others saying I had an ulcerated stomach. I tried five of them, and thPy gave me little relief, the pain al- ways returning. " Then how did you obtain permanent relief ?" (the patient was engaged In a hearty meal when the reporter entered.)

• " Oh, my wife read in the paper of • Dr. William's Pink Pills for Pale People. As soon as I started taking them I found relief. I have never had any pain at the stomach since I took the third box. I eat any- thing now—short cake or pie crust, or anything ; and before that I dare eat nothing of that sort. I seemed to have no blood left, I was so pale. I was so thin I was like a skeleton, and never thought I should live to see the present time ; but I should think I am now two stones heavier." Mr. Dalton also stated that he had been six weeks in the Louth Hospital, without perman- ent relief. "I have been at work ever since I took the Pills," he added, "and I should not have done if without them."

Dr. Williams' Pink Pill:: cure rheumatism, neuralgia, locomotor ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, nervous headache, and prostration; diseases of the blood, such as scrofula, chronic erysipelas, &c., restore pale and sallow com- plexions to the glow of health, are a specific for all the troubles peculiar to the female sex, and in men cure all cases arising from worry, overwork, or excesses. They are sold by all chemists, and by Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, 46, flolborn-viaduct, London,at 2s. 9d.a box, or six for 13s. 9 L,in wooden boxes with pink wrapper; genuine only with full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People.

W EDNES ATHLECTIC SPORTS.

The 28th anniversary in connection with Widnes Athletic Sports was held on Friday evening, on the Widnes Sports Ground, Farnworth. The weather being favourable there was a large gathering of spectators. NOTES kiN GA.MhS AND

ATHLETICS.

CRICKET.

The Warrington team signalised their first appearance against Little Lever by scoring a win by the satisfactory maj)rity of 52 runs. The Little Lever team was with- out their county wicket keeper, C. Smith, hut this was counterkalanced by the fact that Warrington were miuus the services of Lieu. Plomer, who has played so consistently, and W. H. Podmore.

The toss was won by Little Lever, and they were all dismissed for 114. R. Smith had by a very small frac- tion, the best average, for he took four wickets for 35 runs, and Bates three for 25. Croatiald and Connor also had a hand in getting their opponents out. The fielding of the Warrington team was excellent, and Clark was in fine form behind the wickets. Only three extras were scored by Little Lever, and one of these was a wide.

The Warrington team had little trouble in passing their opponents' aggregate, and some excellent scores were made. W. Mortimer came out at the top with 46, though what J. J. Crosfield would have done could he have finished his innings it is difficult to say. It is also gratifying to note that Bate put on the good score of 33. Bind made his re-appearance in the team, and proved that he is a very consistent scorer, for although he has been at the wickets four times. he has only been out

twice. --- The averages of the Warrington team have undergone some change this week. Alfred Clark has passed the century, while R. Smith who has been at the top for so long, is now third, Hind coming first, and J. .1. Crosfield second. R. Smith will very soon have completed his second hundred, and Crosfield is not very far behind.

Below is the present state of the averageg of those who have played in four innings and who have an average of four and over : times

innings not out runs average. H Hind J J Crosfleld R. Smith Lieu. Plenum Clark (A) Bites Wl4 Podmore . 7 E England 3 Callon ()Clarke W Wordsworth . 6 B I onnor

Mr. Fleming., the captain of the Warrington 2nd team has wonderful luck in winning the toss. He has won it on every occasion during the present season. As usual he won it on Saturday, and was thus able to give his side an opportunity of putting on 185 for nine wickets, towards which Adamson scored 55, Brown 47, and Dackworth 36. Little Lever 2nd could only raise 62 in reply owing chiefly to the splendid bowling of Bibby who took five wickets for 27 runs.

The match between Sankey Wesleyans and St. Helens Congregationalists had a very unsatisfactory termina- tion owing to the rain on Saturday. Only nine of the Wealeyans' wickets kad fallen, when the rain came on and stopped all further play. The second team were more fortunate, and succeeded in avenging a previous defeat at toe hand. of 50... Luke's in a very decisive manner by scoring 136 to 44.

The Cairo-st. Unity and the Y.M.C. A. had a tussle on Saturday, and according to the former the result was a draw, but according to the latter it was a win for them. According to the evidence of the umpire, however, the Y.rti C.A. were victorious owing to Patrick being run out just on time.

Laneashire have not been doing so well of late, there- fore,they were not expected to make anything approach- ing a record score just at present. They have,however, astonished everybody by establishing a record in county cricket. by scoring 801 in a single innings at the expense of S mersot. The bowlers of the western county have had very hard times of it lately for towards the close of last week, Essex scored more than 825 against them. The averages of the Somerset bowlers are, therefore, completely spoiled for the present year. At the match against Lancashire, nine bowlers were tried, which shows the straits which the Somerset team we-e put in.

The hero of the match was A. C. Maclaren. This player had not taken part in county cricket since Whit Monday. hut he has quickly made up for lost time alsd scored 424 fora sisgle innings,thus beating the Somerset team himself. lie has also established a recoril in first class cricket., indeed he has occupier/4y cm ished all other records. The Somerset bowlers must have Oven up all hope of getting him out,and when Fowler caught him it was as great a surprise to him as the Torr victory at Derby was to the two Tory candidates. Necdless to say Lancashire won with the greatest of ease. It will pro- bably be a long time before the new record is broken, and it is satisfactory that it is a Lancashire born man who has made it.

SWIMMING.

The match vrith'St. Aug-ustint's in the Liverpool and District Water Polo League has never heea thought of seriously as anything requiring an effort to uphold the honour of the local team, as the former ranks in emin- ence with Wootton who were defeated by 20 goals to nil. A comparatively second rate team was sent on Monday evening when the Warrington and St. Angus- tines players met in the Margaret-st. Baths, Liverpool, hut nevertheless a victory of 14 goals to 1 was gained by the visitor.. Nothing much was done but shoot at goal through which the ball was put by all the Warrington players with the exceptioniof the goal keeper. Both teams however proved themselves fairly matched at the dinner to which the visitors were afterwards entertained.

OUR LOCAL CFIIIROFIES-WARRINGTON.

woRICINGMEN'S MISSION, .BANK QUAY..

Special services were held at the above Mission on Sunday, in order to wipe off a small debt which has accumulated during the past few months. Mr. W. Brocklehurst, of MI ncheßter, occupied the pulpit in the morning and evening,and there were good congregations at each service. Mr. Brocklphurst preached two eloquent sermons, and they were listened to with great interest. A sacred cantata entitled " Buttercups and daisies" was given by the choir and children in the afternoon, at which s-rvice there was again a good attendance. The various pieces were very efficiently rendered and the soloists, etc., were Mrs. Joseph Monks, jun., Miss Ashley. Miss Florrie Rascal, Mr. James Baker and Mr. Samuel HAPS3II. The leader was Mr. J. Mills, and the organist, Mr. F. Atkinson, to which gentlemen great credit is due for the manner in which they had trained the singers. The connective readings were given by Mr. Joseph Monks, jun. The Mission bad been very tastefully decorated with flowers by Mrs. Ashley, Mrs. Joseph Monks, Miss Ashley, and Miss Hassall. The flowers had been supplied by Mr. Hipps at the request of Mr. A. H. Crosfield, sad after the services were over, they were presented to the Infirmary. Collections were taken at each service, and realised a 91.55.

ROYAL LANCASHIRE AGRICULTURAL SOCIETY.

The annual exhibition of the Royal Lancashire Agricultural Society, will be held next Thursday, Friday and Saturday at Preston, and as the entries exceed the total of last year by some hundreds, there is every prospect of its being a success. The following are the particulars of entries of the various sections, the first number being the entries , at the exhibition at Bolton last year,and the second, the entries this year;—Farms, 11, 11; cattle, 169, 151 ; light horses, 290, 399; shire, &c., horses, 158, 146; sheep, 105, 91; goats, 64, 50; potatoes, 6,5; cheese, 45. 80; butter and working dairy, 56, 84; dogs, 195. 194 ; poultry, 463, 495 ; pigeons, 249, 250; honey, 56, 59 ; eggs, nil, 38 ; total entries of live stock and produce, 1867, 2053; number of imple- ment stands, 163, 187 ; total, 2030, 2240.

STOCKTON HEATH.

SAD DEATH OF A CTrILD.—An inquest was held by Mr. Dobson at the Mulberry Tree Inn,on Saturday after- noon, on the body of Elizabeth Shaw, aged 18 months, daughter of George Shaw. The evidence showed that on Wednesday night, at about seven o'clock, Mrs. Tirzeh Shaw, the wife of George Shaw, wheelwright, left her house, 4, Bostock-st., to go to chapel according to her usual custom on Wednesday evenings. She had previ- ously taken her youngest daughter, Elizabeth, to bed, and had left her twelve-year-old son, Richard Turner, in charge of the house. The boy went to look at the child twice after his mother had gone, and had found it lying in bed quite safe. Some time afterwards,he wetif to look at it tor a third time, and found the child lilt ot bed, sitting upon the rug in front of the firegrate. He saw also that a bottle of carbolic acid, uncorked,was in the grate, the cork being on the fender. He scolded the chUd for getting out of bed, and picking her up placed her upon the bed. She began to cry, and then to vomit. Smelling what he thought was carbolic acid, he grew frightened and took his baby sister to a neighbour, who administered a dose of cod liver oil as an emetic. The oil had the de-ired effect, after which the child was taken to Dr. Willett, who likewise gave her an emetic, and ordered her to be bathed in hot water. The mother had ho' this time been sent for from the chapel, but being prostratSd almost with grief was not permitted by the neighbours to minister to the child's requirements until after nine o'clock, when there were all the appearances of much improvement in its condition. The same night Dr. Willett again saw the child, and also on Thursday and Friday. On the after- noon of the latter day, outward convulsions set in, and the child died at twenty minutes past four. No evidence as to the child being seen to drink the poison was forth- coming, but the mother explained to the coroner that she had hurriedly placed the bottle containing the acid, in the bottom firegrate on Wednesday afternoon and had left it there. It was corked when the child had been put to bed. She had used the acid to destroy insects which infested the bedsteads, skirting boards, &c. She ac- knowledged to the coroner, in a tone of deep emotion, that she had been careless in leaving the poison in such a dangerous place. She had at other times put it upon a shelf in the kitchen.—Sergeant Gosling informed the coroner that he had examined the body of the deceased that morning. He had found a slight discolouration on the lips and neck, and the hands were clenched. Hs produced the bottle referred to, and which contained a small quantity of carbolic acid. He had been informed by Mrs. Shaw that only a very small drop of the peison had gone from the bottle since she had used it on Wei nesday afternoon.—The jury thought they had no evi- dence before them to show conclusively that the child administered the poison itself, and accordingly returned a verdict that the child had died from convulsions.

DESTRUCTIVE FIRE AT HAYDOCK.

NARROW ESCAPES.

On Friday afternoon soon after twelve o'clock, a fire broke out at Old Boston, close to Messrs. Evans and Co's private line, and resulted in the entire destruction of seven cottages. The cottages in question are of an old fashioned class, with thatched roofs.and are said to have been built upwards of 200 years. They belong to Lord Newton, and the gable end of a block of four comes within a few feet of Messrs. Evan's line leading to the Colliery. Recently the firm have commenced to run a small engine lup this line, to do the work which was formerly performed by horses, and it is alleged that it was a spark from an engine which set the roof of one of theicottages on fire. An alarm was at ence given,but before any service could be rendered the roofs of the cottages were in flames from end to end. Sparks were scattered on to the adjoining block of three cottages and soon the whole lot was ablaze. A Mrs. Owen who was just re- covering from an illness endeavoured to save some of her furniture, but she found it necessary to make a speedy exit, and with her three children had just crossed the threshold when the burning roof fell in, and the whole of her furniture, was destroyed. In another case a Mr. VValtnsley'was in bed slightly unwell, and in the con- fusion he was forgotten. Almost at the last moment, when the room was something like a furnace, his wife remembered his danger, and at great risk a policeman succeeded in rescuing him. An old man named Bradbury, a confirmed invalid had to be wrapped in blankets and carried from the burning house. A large number of workmen, and a staff of police under Sergant Bags, did what they could to subdue the flimes,but without avail, and they then directed their efforts to save another block of cottages, in which they were more successful. Mr. Barrett (Messrs. Evan's Manager) Mr. Beilly,Mr. George and other officials were quickly at the spot, and every effort was made to remove the furniture of the unfortun- ate people to a safe place. Many of them however lost the greater portion of their effects, and in consequence are put to much inconvenience.
