THE "BUSY BEES" PRODUCTS.

Few people have any idea of the importance of the products ot the " little busy bee." In the United States we learn from the " American European News Letter.' there are 2,800,000 stocks of bees, which annually pro- done 30,000 tons of honey, value 4,140,0001015. £B2B 000), and 5,500 tons of wax, worth 2,475,0001°1e. (£495,000). The latest figures for the countries of Con- tinental Europe show an annual output of wax of about 15.000 tons with a value approachihg £1,350,000. Of honey the annnal production is estimated at 80,000 tons, and its vslue at £2,200,000. Not a bad show as a result of " improving the shining hours."

===== 0002601_18950720_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
EXTRAORDINARY PREDICTIONS.

When the late Countess of Moray was twelve years old, and a Miss Lockhart, of Carnwalt in Lanarkshire, a gipsy foretold that she would have two husbands, and that just before her death she would pass through a newly-felled wood, and be drawn in a carriage by a pie- bald horse to the house from which she would never come alive. The prophecy was very strangely realised. Miss Lockhart's first husband was the Earl of Aboyne ; her second the Earl of Moray; shortly before her death she visited the Highlands of Scotland, and passed through the forest of Glenmore, then in process of re- moval, and on the following morning the coachman came to say that one of her four horses had died suddenly in the night, and that he had therefore borrowed another. It was a piebald onel She went on to Culloden House, where she was taken ill, and after a short time expired. It is recorded of the poet Dryden, by Charlos Wilson, in his "Life on Congreve," that having, strange to say, belief in astrology, he was careful to ascertain to the second the time at which his son Charles was born. He then calculated the I.oy'a nativity, and was alarmed to discover that evil influences prevailed in the heavens: Jupiter, Venus, and the sun being below the earth, and the lord of his ascendant afflicted with a baleful square of Mars and Saturn. He concluded that in his eighth year, and on the day of birth, his son's life would be seriously endangered if not lost; and that if he lived,the same danger would exist when he attained his twenty- third birthday, and again on his thirty-third or fourth. On the boy's eighth birthday, despite every precaution to keep the boy from every possible danger, he was nearly killed by the fall of a wall. On his twenty-third birthday he was seized with giddiness and fell from an old tower belonging to the Vatican at Rome; and he was drowned at Windsor while swimming across the Thames in his thirty-third year.

===== 0002601_18950720_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY DOES HE CARRY A CANE?

The subject of why Tommy Atkins carries a cane has been investigated by a correspondent of " Cassell's Saturday Journal," who managed recently to buttonhole a communicative private. " Well, it's this way," said the soldier. "When we've got something in our bands it prevents us sleuthing as a good many civilians do. I don't know why, but a swagger cane always make a soldier look smart. Who first thought of it ? I can't tell you, but drill-sergeants have always carried sticks, and they use 'em, too, and so does the drum-major. and I suppose that's how it came about. Or it may be that the smart look of the cavalrymen wit n their whips sug- gested it." "Do you have to buy your sticks ? " " Well, it depends upon the regiment,. Some regiments provide them. When a man joins he has a stick given him, and if he loses it he has to buy another. Of course he can give what price he likes, but if he's hard up the canteen caterer will let him have one for three- pence or so. Not many take up with these cheap canes, though." " What about the officers ?" " Oh, they have to have sticks just the same. The commissioned fficers of couree go in for expensive ones, and the non- coms, they choose good substantial sticks ; we privates generally like a light cane. It don't make much differ- ence to the regulations what stick you have so long as it's a stick ot some sort. When we go out—that is, in walking-out order, you understand—the guard would challenge us if we didn't have a stick." " Why not have a regulation stick 2" " Yes ; some regiments have sticks with the badge of the regiment on the metal head. We should all like thA t, and you may depend upon it we should stick to our sticks."

===== 0002601_18950720_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ADVENTURE WITH PIRATES.

Captain Raiford, with his ship, the " Eliza," recently had a practical demonstration of the fact that the high seas are not quite clear of pirates. While bound from Amoy to Hong Kong, the" Eliza " was boarded by about 200 pirates, headed by a Mexican named Alvarez. The crew of the " Eiza," after a stubborn resistance, were disarmed and confined in the chain locker, with the exception of the ca ptain.carpenter,and an apprentice lad. Alvarez shot the captain three times, first in the head, but the bullet glanced round the skull., then in the arm and side. The carpenter and apprentice took re- fuge in the foretop. The pirates left the captain for dead, looted the cabin and, after making a hole in the bottom of the vessel, departed,takbag Mrs. Raiford with them. Meanwhile the carpenter and boy came down the rigging and, releasing the rest of the crew, managed t get a boat before the ship sank. The captain was only stunned, and was taken into the boat. After ex- periencing some suffering for want of food and water they were picked up by the steamer " Venitzia." and taken to Hong Kong. As soon as Captain Raiford recovered from his wounds he disguised himself as a Chinaman, and, as he spoke the language fluently, this was not difficult. flis obi.ct was to find his wife. For three weeks lie travelled about, and at last found that she had been sold as a slave by the Mexican to a rich mandarin. With the aid of two English detectives he bought her back for £4O. Alvarez was afterwards arrested by the British authorities at Hong Bong, and tried and condemned as a pirate. Out of consideration for the fact that he had been an officer in the army, he was shot instead of being hanged.

TRADE IN LANCASHIRE.

The following reports are from the July number of the " Labour Gaz-tt ;—ln Manchester and district cotton spinning continues moderate, but the mules are working more regularly, and at Macclesfield, employment is still good. The si k trade shows no decline, dressers, dyers, and finishers being fully employed. The improvement in the engineering tiade of Manchester and district continues, wi' h a small reduction in the number of un- employed. Brass founders moderate; braziers good ; the wiredrawers and wire-weavers of Warrington fair. The tailoring trade is lair, though in the whole sale department it has declined. Cape makers are busy ; ready-made mantle and shirt makers and water proof garment and umbrella makers dull. The felt hat trade is slack, only firms employed on ladies' hats tieing belay. Employment in the builciing trade is reported good in Manchester, and moderate at Stockport. The upholsterers report employment as good, and the Ca beet makers and coopers are busy. Coach-making in Manchester and Warrington is good. In the printing and allied trades letterpress printers re- port trade as bad, with five per cent. unemployed. Lithographic printers and bookbinders dull. In Steck- port printers are busy, and in some cases working over- time. The salt works at Northwich are still working short time. In Oldham and district there is no im- provement in the cotton spinning industry. Of the 20,892 spinners and cardroom operatives making re- . turns, 1,450 (or 7 per cent.) are receiving unemployed benefit. Weavers, winders, and ring-frame tenters are well employed ; twiners and reelers only moderate. In Stalybridge one mill is being worked three days per week. The engineers report employment as in derate. In the machine shops the workmen are better employed. Boiler-makers, Ironfounders, &c., are well employed. In the coalmines employment is only moderate. In Bolton and district the spinning branch of the cotton trade remains de- pressed. Employment is irregular, some mills being on short time, and 72 members receiving out of work pay as compared with 57 at the end of May. Weaving con- tinues normal. In Bury, the spinners, cardroom opera- tives, and weavers report employment as dull, few mills working full time, and many being stopped. In Chor- ley employment is irregular. In Bolton engineering has improved. Some departments are only moderately busy. Steel-smelting is depressed. In Wigan the en- gineers are busy ; the iron-rolling mills are working full time. Steel-r(elling is depressed ; smelters moderate. In Bury engineering is slack. Machine shops are busy. A slight imprevement is reported in the Wigan coal industry, most of the colliers working an extra day per week-. The building trade of Bolton is unusually active. Wigan and Bury are fairly good ; unsettled in Chorley. In Burnley, Accrington, and dis- trict the weaving trade has slightly improved, fewer looms being stopped for warps, but in the Blackburn district several weavers are unemployed, owing to the stoppage of firms. The sateen trade is worse again. Coloured goods contibue fair. In the spinning industry there is little change in the Accrington district, although full time i 3 being worked. In the surrounding localities few men are totally unemployed. In the engineering and metal trad;3 machine making continues good, and turners, millwrights, !cc., are fully employed. The building trade continues good ; calico printing and dye- ing fairy good.

ENGLISH POLITICS.

(BY Mn. H. WHITHAM.) Certainly the unforeseen does happen. A week ago I saw in an English paper just received that "the rumours of an almost immediate dissolu- tion of Parliament, are not likely to be verified by the event." Several days previous to reading this the telegraph had announced to all the world the fall of the Rosebery Government. and the conse- quent journeying of Salisbury to Windsor. The fall of the Liberals from power has seemed for weeks past to be imminent. That it would come about by a scratch vote did not appear so certain, neither party liking the idea of rushing into the turmoil and excitement of a general elec- tion just at this time. And yet so long as Mr. Chamberlain remains a bird of prey, as it were, ready at any time to fall upon friend or foe, he will be the dark horse of politics. T.he orchid must he in its fullest bloom and splendour at all times whatever else may fade away. Salisbury and Balfour have had an object lesson j as Gladstone and the Irish had before them. And yet there may be some hope for the people out of the heterogenious atoms out of which a Coalition Government will be formed. All Union- ists are not Chamberlains. Some among them have shown during recent months their utter inability to carry the Tory gag quietly. In the framing of a policy honest men will not allow the bugaboo of disunion to frighten out of their heads all mention of measures necesary for the peace and the per- manency of Great Britain. So it may come about that the staid and steady Tory whose only policy is "do nothing" will have plenty oflopportunity to do some " kicking " as a result of which maybe the Liberal Party will gain its own, and Chamberlain will have time for studying English politics as they are in this latter end of the 19th century. Reformers have, all through the ages, fought and shed their blood for the freedom of man. The liberation of his mental activities has, within our days, been accompqnied, or, has been coincident with the demand for equal representation, always keeping in mind the tact that fair and equal taxa- tion could only result from the breaking of aristo- cratic and plutocratic legislation. What can a workingman expect from a Salisbury—Devonshire —Balfour Government, with the unknown— quantity thrown in as a menace? Europe and America look for great things from the new Government. Germany and Russia hope to be aided in their schemes of repression. A Liberal Government in I+ ngland is dreaded by these patern- ally governed countries. They know' and do not hesitate to say it aloud, that the work of the pro- gressive party in England stands out clear and definite as an object lesson to their dissatisfied sub- jects, and causes them serious trouble ; therefore, they hail the accession to power of the Tory party with joy. Germany with America now looks forward with some hope to a monetary conference, the former country not hoping much from an international talk on the matter, but seeing clear that something must be done to pacify the Irreconcilables. The latter country looks to a conference with the keenest interest. It has silver to sell and looks to England for her biggest customer. The apprecia- tion of silver would, America thinks, take her at once out of all her troubles. The double standard is all the talk here in the West. It is not so much so in the Eastern States. During the past three years as much mud has been slung at Britannia as ought by this time to have completely soiled her fair form. And yet what is the position ? Eng- land maintains the single, monometallic, or gold standard. Until now she has seemed to prosper with it. From the sheer force of circumstances, I suppose, the other countries have also had the gold standard. History records that at various periods changes have been made, and the bi-metallic, or gold and silver standard was tried, but the encour- agement was not sufficient to continu3 it, and so the single standard was reverted 80. The conse- quence of all this has been, so it is alleged, that gold has 80 appreciated, or risen in value as to cripple all trade, and thus bring about the everlast- ing labour question. It is very likely that what satisfies London mer- chants and money brokers is not the right article to satisfy the hungry millions. I grant thia,but I also assert with Sir M. Hicks-Beach, who in the recent speech at Bristol, said.:—" He thought those mat- ters on which were based party politics and on which they differed of great moment, and so they were, but he would venture to tell them that a mistake in dealing with the currency in this country might bring on as much ruin to Great Britain as any political change in contemplation at the present time could inflict. Therefore be was surprised at the facility with which many gentle- men, who, he thought, had not studied this matter more carefully than he had done, pronounced their opinions for or against monometallism or bimetal- lism as if they were able masters of the subject." how, this is an honest statement of the case, and should the Tory candidate at the coming election claim to stand on a double standard platform he will claim what no Tory Government will at the present go in for. There is an anti-silver sentiment in London the Tories will respect. . . _ - .

Sir Charles Lilke speaking' recently said, " the Liberals succeeded in winning the hatred and opposition of every interest that was threatened without satisfying the masses, in whose behalf the reforms were proposed." This, perhaps, is the ex- planation of the fall of the party. The statement deserves close study from the workingmen, because such a studied sentence could never be ' lormed as the reason for the downfall of a Tory Government. In other words, it weans that the Liberal Government has, in its anxiety to benefit the toilers antagonized the wealthy classes, and now the rich monopolist hopes to have a govern- ment that will protect his wealth from all socialist schemes. That the Liberals would have done some- thing towards satisfying the toilers had it not been' for the steady majority arrayed against it in the Howe of Lords and the existence of a powerful faction in the Commons goes without saying. It! is very likely that before these lines are read a; Conservative policy will have been put before the county and that a general election will be in full swing. As yet no indication of what that policy will be has appeared beyond some milk and water legislation for Ireland, which may not be accept- able to the new Irish allies of the Tories. The Redmond party may decrease in strength, and as they do so, the Salisbury indifference to Ireland's grievances will increase and so the fun will go on merrily. The Liberals commenced work with a tolerable programme. It may have contained too much, and they may have here accomplished too little. No doubt such startling propositions as the abolition or reformation of the house of Lords, Home Rule for Ireland, the further extension of the franchise by means of reformed registration, the political emancipation of women, the abolition of state churches and the confiscation of their revenues for ' national purposes, and the control of the liquor traffic, are measures enough to frighten those who think things are best as they are,but certainly the I.L.P. and reformers generally would have been pleased to-day had such measures been now on the statue books.

Socialists, I.L.P's and all who take an active inter- est in measures tending to ameliorate the condition of the people will do well at such a juncture as this to study out for themselves which way tneir vote ought to go. For myself 1 have given over hoping to see anything politically good come from tbe people. The stream of good things will nave to come down hill; we shall never make it run up hill. Political self-sacrifice is not a characteristic of the people ; therefore we must make use of the materials we have at band.

June 280, 1895, 27f-13th Street, San Francisco.

PAINFUL TRAGEDY AT JARROW.

At Durham yesterday Eliza Urquhart, 54, was in- dicted for murdering her husband at Yarrow. The de- ceased was bed-ridden and a great sufferer, and as a son who had supported them was out of work and was unable to provide for them, Mrs. Urquhart, with the husband's knowledge and sanction, to put him out of pain, gave him some rat poison, which killed him in fifteen minutes. She afterwards endeavcured to drink some poibon herself, but was prevented. The jury found her guilty, but decided that she was not responsible tor her actions, and she was ordered to be detained during Her Majesty's pleasure.

THE EXAMINER, SAT URDAY, JULY 20, 1895.

victor. tettero to

.A. WORKING-3IAN'S VIEW OF LIBERALISYL Sir,—If you think my epistle is worth a place in your valuable paper, and it may persuade only a few Liberal voters to remain strong and stand true to the noble cause of Liberalism,l shall be glad. Well, Sir, when I was only a boy I began to take an interest in politics and saw a great difference between the two great political parties. Being one of the toiling ones, I wondered which party cared for and sought the working-naan's interest the most. There were then a number of measures presented before the country as the respective politics of the two great parties of State, the Liberal party pressing forward the wheel of progress, and considering the wrongs and dis- abilities, and grievances under which men were placed. It did not take roe long to see ray best friend ,and I soon became a confirmed Liberal, and have many reasons for being so. I have lived long enough to see not only the Liberal party advocating the working- man's cause and his interests, political, social, and rel- igious, but also accomplishiug much on his behalf. I need not name the measures which have been passed this last 30 years, for they are well-known. I remember some years ago Mr. Jacob Bright, M.P., giving an address in the Public flail. lie said be could 'sot understand a working-man being a Conservative. Neither can I. If a man will read history and think for himself and be honest to his conscience he cannot be a Conservative. By reason of my environment lam a , Nonconformist, and the people called Dissenters suffered , long under the yoke of oppression and tyranny. I find , that the Liberals have passed, or caused to be passed, roost of the laws which have contributed to the peace arid well-being of the working-man, thereby proving beyond question the fact, viz., that they are the best friend, of the working-class. . it the same time, working-men should ever remember this, that the Tory party have always opposed with great determination the passing of all these measures into law. The Tory party have always been on the side of oppression and tyranny. We Liberals need to unite our forces, and do our utmost to again press forward those great measures of reform, contained in their policy,. Mr. Uladstone's advice must be carried out—we must not be devided. Roping and trusting we shall see our wishes realized.-1 SIM, yours truly.

P R OGRESS.

- THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

The "Things Heard and Seen" this week are with I regard to the electoral battle now being waged in ' our midst, for it is the topic which eclipses all others at the present time. That the Tories are mak- ing a desperate effort to wrest the seat from the Liberals there can be no doubt, and they are buoyed up with hope through the reverses the Liberals , have sustained in other 9arts of the Country. Run- corn has usually been considered the stronghold of the Lib' al party of the division and it is here that the Tories are fighting desperately. One of their cries has been bad trade which is attributed to the Liberal Government. This has been a catching cry throughout the country and has no doubt had a great deal to do with the success of the party who have issued it. But probably no one knows better than Mr. Ward himself that governments have very little effect upon trade, and further, he knows, or ought to know, that long before the Liberal Government came into power in 18n, trade had been on the decline.

-- Sir John Brunner at his meetings has favourably alluded to this mendacious Statement of the Liberal Government being responsible for bad trade, and as a business man his words are worthy of con- sideration. He attributes had trade largely to the rash and extravagant speculations of Messrs. Bar- ing Bros. The crash of that large trading house in 1800 (when the Tories were in power) he affirms, caused everybody to sell securities in a great hurry; the stock-markets came down with a rush and everybody felt insecure in business, none would go into speculation, and everybody called in all debts it was possible to collect. This seems a more sensi- ble explanation than to foist the blame on any Government, and Sir John Brunner's question as to whether the Tory or Liberal Government could be held responsible for reckless trading is a nut which the Tories will find hard to crack.

But there is another remark regarding the trade of Runcorn which needs to be dealt witn. It is one which Mr. Charles Reynolds made at a Tory meeting in Runcorn. Now Mr. Reynolds says he is a Liberal Unionist and one would think he would be more likely to speak upon Home Rule than upon a personal matter concerning Mr. Brunner's works. They must be aware," he said, "that the company Sir John Brunner represented had knocked the bottom out of the trade of Runcorn." Ile was referring to the alkali trade which as everyone knows is at a very low ebb in Runcorn. This remark was followed up by Mr. Hornby Lewis who went a little further—or at least went on to explain what Mr. Reynolds meant. He informed the audi- ence on Wednesday evening, that it was owing to the competition of Messrs. Brunner, Mond and C.)mpany that the United Alkali Company had had to discharge a large number of men at Runcorn. Now the question naturally arises, if that is so, why did it not occur when the works in Run- corn were under private ownership? There was no lack of work then at the Runcorn works and the same competition had to be met. There seems only one conclusion and that is, that the chemical ring have raised their prices to such an extent that they have been cut out of the market in many places. The experience of the Runcorn people regarding the United Alkali Company has been, up to the present, that they have not much to thank them for.

The meeting on Wednegday evening at which these statements were made was not one upon which the Conservatives or so-called Unionists could congratula*e themselves upon. The best feature of it was the speech of the candidate who stated his claims in s fair and straightforward manner. But the vote pledging the meeting to support Mr. Ward was very doubtfully carried, in fact to an unprejudiced onlooker it seemed to have been lost.

The Northwich polling (lay is on Wednesday next and that day must see the Liberals win. They have the best candidate, the most complete or- ganization, the canvass augurs well for success,and all that is wanted to ensure victory is a hand of voluntary workers who will put their shoulders to the wheel end abate not one jot until the closing of the poll. There most he no half-heartedness about it, because there has been a big majority before— Derby roust, be remembered and care taken,that the same result is not repeated at Northwich. "so let's close up our ranks, show firm front to foe, For faithful and true is our Member ; Let no Tory lonk in, hack the Tory most go, For faithful and true is our Member. She!l we give up Northw3c;l? Ten tlrroand times no ! Let's add to our number and mak“ it to glow For all time as a beacon, the Tories to show We're faithful and true to our Member.

EARLESTOWN A.ND NEWTON.

NEWTON RACES.

The annual race meeting at Newton commences 1 to-day (Friday) and will be continued on Saturday. All the works in the district ceased operations on Thursday night and will remain closed in the case of the Vulcan and Viaduct Works, until Tuesday morning, in consequence of the election on Monday.

POLICE CEANGES.

In connection with the proposed augmentation of the police force in the Newton district, PC. Clarke, of Birkdale, has been promoted to sergeant, and removed to Earlestown. It is also expected that two or three constables will also be drafted here.

Crozier, J. Evans, W. D. Johnson, J. C. Seddon, E. Fair. BOWLING HANDICAP. On Saturday last a handicap at bowls was played on the Institute Club green, for Mr. E. T. Evans' annual prize. There were 34 entries, the first prize being secured by Mr. T.Compton. The second prize, an umbrella,was taken by Mr. James Bedworth,and the third prize, pipe and case, by Mr. W. Tarbuck. ' The prizes were distributed by the Secretary, Mr. James Allen, and a hearty vote of thanks was accorded Mr. Evans. EXCURSION FROM HA.YDOCK. The annual excursion of Messrs. Evans and Co.'s employes, took place on Saturday. Four long trains, containing altogether something like 3,000 people left Haydock at various intervals between six and seven o'clock for Blackpool, which they reached about nine o'clock. The excursionists im- mediately dispersed to enjoy themselves after their individual inclinations, and the day being fine,they had a most pleasant outing. They returned home at various times, the last train reaching Haydock about two o'clock. GOOD TEMPLAR/4. The usual weekly session of the Hope of Earles- town Lodge of Good Templars was held in the Co- operative Hall, on Monday evening,when there was a very good attendance of members, Bro. Wilson, C.T., presiding. Bro. E. Newman, L.D., reported on the monthly session of No.. 1 Convention held at Leigh on the previous Saturday. Several other items of business were transacted, after which the programme of the evening was proceeded with. A very interesting selection of songs, recitations, and dialogues was rendered in a very satisfactory man- ner. On Wednesday evening a party of the mem- bers numbering 32 proceeded by train to Tranmere, Cheshire, to pay an arranged visit to the Gleam of Sunshine Lodge—the largest Good Templar Lodge in the world. The visitors arrived at their destina- tion about 730, and were heartily welcomed. After 'being entertained to an excellently-served supper they were introduced with honours. Bro. George Clarke, P.D. Co..presided,and was supported by Bro. E. Newman, D.S.J.T. Bro. W. Wilson, C.T., Bro. Sergeant Roberts, of W'arrington, and Bro. T. Pool, D.O T.,other offices in the lodge being filled by visit- ing members. After a few remarks from the Chairman, an excellent programme was gone through in a manner to call forth the hearty appreciation of the large audience present. Solos were given by Sisters Miss Roberts, Miss A. Newman, NI rs. E. Whitty, and Mrs. Willis,and Bro. W. 11. Crowther; recitations were given by Sister R. Warburton, Bros. W. Wilson, and J. Hopkins,and a dialogue by Bros. W. IN ilson and George Clarke. Bro. W. Willis who accompanied the soloists very efficiently, gave a splendid rendering of the March (piano) " Siege of Paris." Bro. Sergeant Roberts gave an earnest and encouraging address. Hearty thanks were accorded the visitors for their e cel- lent programme, and Bros. W. 11. Dale and E. New- man briefly responded. The meeting' then concluded the visitors arriving home safely about 12 p.m ,after a very pleasant and enjoyable outing. DISTRICT SCHOOL.—PRIgH DISTRIBUTION. The annual prize distribution took place at this school on Friday last—additional interest being taken in the proceedings from the fact that the Hon. T. W. Legh spared a short time from his electioneering campaign to attend and present the prizes to the scholars who had been fortunate enough to win them. Prizes are awarded for attendance (all who have not been absent more than one half day in the course of the school year being entitled to one) and for merit in the different subjects taught in the school. The large number of 951 in the mixed department alone, had qualified for an attendance prize. In addition to the above prizes Dr. Flandysids annually gives two special progress prizes for which there is keen competition. Alice Bailey and Herbert Walton had the good fortune to become the receipients of these on Fri- day. It is worthy of note that 225 children attended school 400 times out of a possible 433—an abnormally good attendance. Punctually ut two o'clock the Hon. T. W. Legh, accompanied by Dr. and Mrs. Handyside and Mr. C. B. F. Borrrn, J.P., entered the school and was received with a round of applause b the children assemberl. There were also present—the Rev. T. Ti. Matthews. the Rev. W. W. Kaye, Messrs. Att field and Peaks, Mesdames Peake, Robinson, and many others. Dr. Handyside, in a few appropriate words introduced the Hon. T. W. Legh, who, previous to the distribution, congratulated the winners and spoke encouraging words to the less fortunate. Ile said it afforded him great pleasure to escape from the din of electoral strife to engage in so pleasur- able an office. He was delighted to learn, from the testimony ef P.M. Inspectors and others, of the highly efficient condition of the school, which re- flected great credit on the managers and teachers and all concerned. (Applause.) Mrs. Mindy-side presented the prizes to the infant scholars, saying words of commendation to the successful pupils. Mr. Peake and Mr. Attfield briefly addressed the little ones. Miss Rodgers, on the occasion of her leaving the school after some years of excellent work, was presented by the scholars and teachers with a handsome dress ring, in recognition of her services and of the high esteem in which she was held by all at tile school.

DEATH OF COLONEL WCORQUODALR

We deeply regret to announce the death of Colonel G. M'Corquodale, 17. D., which took place at half-past five o'clock on Tuesday afternoon, at his Welsh residence, GadlYg. Ilansadwrn, Isle of Anglesey, in his seventy- ninth year. He had been ill for some time, and though his death was not unlocked for, it will cause a feeling of sorrow throughout aWirle circle.the deceased having been for many years a notable public man. When the sad news became known at Newtonde-Willows, where the deceased had for many years resided, and where the principal wouks of M'Corquodale and Co., Limited, are situated; the deepest regret was expressed at the loss of so great a benefactor of the district. The deceased gentleman was a native of Liverpool, and here it VMS that he laid the foundation of 11i9 gigan- tic business upwards of half a century ago. Possessing , those essential qualificatinos for a successful business; !career, he was shrewd, prompt, thormaL;h. and enter- prising.and was known at home and abroad as a man of high commercial rectitude The late Mr. B. Guinness Orchard, in his tflok, "Liverpool Laglon of Honour." published in 1893, stated that Colonel M'Corquodale's successful, useful, and honourable business career had made his fellow-townsmen proud of him, the more so because in rising from the bottom to the top he was emphatically self-made, and owed less to strange Acci- dents or strong patrens than to systematic industry, punctuality, and those rarer faculties, organising talent and clear perception of men and circumstances. George M'Corquodale started earlv in business with a little place in Orange-court. Castle-st., and as a printer and stationer soon acquired an excellent connection. This he expanded in a manner seldom witneved, even in Liver- pool. He secured the printing business of the London and North-Western Railway, and other huge contracts followed—each sought for, caught, and executed with masterly skill. In a few years he had extensive print- ing works here and in London, LNeda, Glasgow, Wolver- ton, and Newton-le•Willows, and had become is paper maker on a large scale. His business talents were so conspicuous that ere he had reached middle life he was talked of throughout the kingdom, as the representative man of both these trades. Even then his advice was widely sought, strong confidence in his general lodgment prevailing. He became a J.P. for Lancashire in 1859, deputy-lieutenant in 1880, and dur- ing a quarter of a century had been one of the trustees far "Bradshaw's Railway Guide." It was notable that Mr. M'Corquodale's success was achieved amidst univer- sal goodwill, for it was hits happy fortune to easily make and keep friends as well as wealth. His people remained With him, some rising from apprenticeship to high mana- gerial posts, and there never was trouble amongst the crowd of subordinates who had such confidence alike in his kindly feelings and his practical wisdom that they cheerfully fell in with his arrangements. Numerous as had been the calls on his time by business and in social ireles where he wsis always welcome, Mr. M'Corquodale •

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 20, 1895

still had leisure, for he was a man of method, who could choose his deputies so sagaciously that all went on right in his absence. Thus when the 'Volunteer force was organised his became lieutenant-colonel of the Liverpool Press Guards, and retained the post until 1868, when on ' retiring from active service he became its honorary colonel. He was an enthusiastic yachtsman, never hap- pier or more genial than when entertaining a party of guests on salt water. Moreover, he had a shooting-box in Scotland, where, before age set in, he was often found in pursuit of game. Latterly, however he spent most of his time at Newton-le-Willows, where he had resided for close on fifty years. Although bearing the weight of more than three-quarters of a eentury;he still was alert shrewd, and master of all that concerned him. One of the most notable events in the career of Colonel M'Corquodale was his connection with the Volunteer force. He was one of the founders, in 1860, of the old Liverpool Press Guard of Volunteers, of which he was colonel. The corps consisted of men engaged in the var- ious branches of press work, and was an ornament to the Volunteer organisation ot the country. The offshoot is the 6th Volunteer Battalion Liverpool Regiment, which however can claim none of the distinctiveness in coin- position of the old Press Guaid. In 1882, Colonel M'Corquodale filled the honourable post of High Sheriff of Lancashire, and he has since oc- cupied a similar position for the county of Anglesey. North Wales. He was also, as stated above. a Deputy- Lieutenant of Lancashire, and a magistrate for the West Derby Hundred, his commission for which dates as far back as 1859. He was twice married, and leaves a widow, five sons, and seven daughters to mourn him.

RECEPTION OF THE NEWS AT NEWTON.

- The reception of the news in Newton caused a very painful impression, in almost every cottage the window blinds were lowered. For some time after the sad news came to band the bell of St. Peter's Church was tolled, and reference was made to the death at the meeting of Mr. Moon's supporters which was being held in the Town Ball. His death was not altogether unexpected, for on Monday information was received that he was in a most critical condition. It appears that Colonel McCorquo- dale was taken seriously ill in London about two months ago, but he recovered and was again apparently iia his usual health. About a month ago however, during the hot weather, he was yachting in the Menai Straits, and he had a sunstroke. He again rallied, but on Sunday week he had a severe attack of bronchitis, and despite the constant attention of Dr. Fenton, of London, and Dr. Dobie, of Chester, he never rallied and died at 5.30 on Tuesday night. The news was wired to Colonel Wood, who lost no time in apprising all those whom he considered were entitled to the information. As a great benefactor of the church, Colonel McCorquodale will be much missed at Newton, and the many charities to which he was a generous subscriber will feel his decease. Be was an enthusiastic yachtsman, and in his time has been the owner of some very fine sailing craft. He has been twice married, his second wife who outlives him being the daughter of the late Canon Saunderson, and he leaves a large family of grown up sons and daughters. The funeral will take place at one o'clock on Saturday at Llansadwrn Churchyard, Gadlys, near Beaumaris.

"A MIGHTY MONARCH."

A Music(h)al(l) Idyll on King Booze ; on the con- sistency of a Pretended Representive, but a really Feudal or Hereditary, system of Government, and other Cognate and Autograph Caligraphies. (Tory Papers please not copy).

Hail to Old Booze! the Great King Booze ! a mighty

monarch he— More potent than Cresars on the land, or Ti-kings on the sea !

Ere makes our vGting millions for their own enslavement

go, And uphold the Non-Elective Gang to govern us, you know.

By the nose he leads his people, as though reason had no mission, And blinds his thoughtless slaves to their ridiculous position— Their voting into power and place of the party who maintain, That Dark-Age Feudal Government must e'er with us . .

remain !- Had I but so much " oof," I'd give ten-thousand pounds to know What treed are our Elections, with their pageantry and show— Of what avail our Franchise or the House of Commons —eh? While the irresponsible House of Lords can have the FINAL SAY ! It is so clearly plain to all who do no shut their eyes, That we might jus, as well possess no Commons or

Franchise; But let the Feudal horde alone in toto make our lawn, For as they're our FINAL ARBITERS no REAL change would cause ! Then all Elections and M.P.'s expenses we could save. No brib'res or intimidations would us then deprave— Then would the fiction that we're Representatively ruled, Be exposed, in all its speciousness, and we no more thus fooled.

So here's to Booze, the Great King Booze ! and his hench- mar, Colonel Bung ! 'Tis unbefitting that such prowess great should go unsung. Were it not for their dread influence, I'm certain as I prattle, Far different wo )Id the outcome be of our Election battle !

Warrington, July, 1891.

STOCKTON HEATH.

A Btrrouen AND HER CUSTOMER.—At the Warrington County Court on Thursday before His Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes Elizabeth Millington butcher, Stockton Heath, sued Stephen Cork, Hollingwood, near Oldham, for £sl4s. sd. for butchers' meat supplied to him when he lived at Stockton Heath. —Mr. Browne appeared for plaintiff and explained that !since the issue of the summons the defendant's wife had paid 83.51. and the defendant now disputed owing any- thin. he defendant himself was often present when the wife gave the orders for butchers' meat and plaintiff delivered a bill to him when the balance was only £4 some months ago. Defendant then produced a receipt for £1 ss. 5. Ills Honour asked him if his wife was present and defendant said she was not. He also pro- duced another receipt for 3s. which the plaintiff con- sented to allow, His Honour therefore gave a verdict for £5 35.. to be paid at £1 a week.

_ THEFT BY A GIRL.—EIiZI Oakes, 15 years of age, of Newton, was charged before Mr. C. The mother of the girl said she was incor- rigible.—The magistrates ordered the girl to be sent to the Workhouse for ten days and arrangements made for her to be sent to some institution.

NEWTON DIVISION ELECTION.

There has been no relaxation in the energy of the two candidates in this district, and the fight is being continued with the sustained vigour. Numer- ous meetings have been held all over the division, by both sides, but the Conservatives, have only con- descended to visit Earlestown, once, although it is considered to be the stronghold of Liberalism. Mr. Moon has been well received, and even at Newton which is supposed to be the nursery of Toryism, he had a packed meeting, and the enthusiasm was in- tense. All along, the fight has been conducted in remarkably good temper; and there has been an absence of personalties. Mr. Naylor, a Warrington teetotaler, was speaking at a Conservative meeting in Newton on Monday night, and he rather ridi- culed the idea which we put forth last week, that Mr. Moon's prospects were so bright, that there seemed a likelihood of the Conservative majority being wiped out. The suggestion seemed to amuse the audience, but as Mr. Rawlins said at another meeting, the Liberals next Monday night may have a story to tell their opponents which they may not like. The walls in the town are bare of mural

literature, the only election indications being placards with the words, "Vote for Moon." In Earlestown the voting will be in wards, and the polling stations will be, For Wargrave and Sugar Works, at Wargrave Schools. _ _

For Viaduct Ward, at lock-up shops near Earles- town Station.

For Town Hall Ward, at District Schools. For Crow-lane Ward, at Wesleyan Day Schools. For Newton Ward, at Town Hall, Newton. . .

The counting will take place at the Town Hall, Newton, and the result will be declared the same night.

GREAT LIBERAL MEETING AT EARLESTOWN. A crowded and enthusiastic meeting of Liberals was held in the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Saturday even- ing, in support of the candidature of Mr. James Moon. Mr. G. W. Rawlins presided, and he was supported by Mr. James Moon, Revs. E. Lloyd Jones (Rhyl), Messrs. W. H rdshaw (Manchester). E Evans, jun. (Liverpool), E. T. Evans, G. F. Clarke, J. W. Emmett, W. Gilmour, C. W. Emmett, and others. The Chairman said that meeting testified to the great enthusiasm Earlestown felt in the cause of Liberalism, and the candidature of Mr. Moon. (Cheers.) During the coming week the battle would rage far harder than it had done during the past week. Mr. Moon had at- tended a large number of meetings; they had seen a great deal of enthusiasm about, the canvassers had been busy and the other side had been busy also. Their on. portents feared the result of the battle, and he was sorry to see in some of the local press a good deal of misrepre- sentation, and some remarks which were in very had taste. One remark that was made, was, that Mr. Moon had attended a meeting at Rainhill, and there were about 25 or 30 people present, but they were members of the Committee, and of workers, and it was not a public meeting at all. (Applause.) Mr. Moon was going to a Public meeting at Rainhill onMonday evening and there would he more than 25 people present. (Hear, hear.) There was a great deal in Mr. Legh's address that he could not swallow, and there was a good deal more if he did swallow, he could not digest. (Laughter.) There was a great deal in it that he could not under- stand, and also something that made one think of it as a Lewis's balloon. (Laughter.) This balloon was large and fine, and painted a blue colour, and they thought there was a good deal in it, but when they pricked it,it collapsed altozether,and when they analysed Mr. Legh'i address, it contained nothing. The other night Mr. Legh had a meeting at Rainhill, and he was asked by an elector present,what he meant by temperance reform. He said he did not know what he meant by it ; he was waiting to see what the Government said, and he would support the Government in the policy that they put forth. That was not the sort of opinion that Mr. Moon held on any question, and he did not think it was the sort of opinion that they wanted their representatives in the House of Commons to held on any question. They did not want a jelly fish condition of man,they wanted 139111e- thing detertnined,a man who worked for the people ,and knew what he meant. (Applause.) Mr. Moen said, after the experience of last week, al- though it had been a hard time he was sure that the flowing tide was with them in Newton, and if his sup- porters would only keep up their enthusiasm they should have a story to tell their opponents that they would not like. (Applause.) He was glad to find that the electors were not using personalities, but that everybody sec riled to he working in good temper. Perhaps they ought to exclude the journal to which Mr. Rawlins had referred, and which that day came out with such remarks. Last week it stated that Mr. Moon had nothing to lose,becauae a Radical luminary at Liverpool was at his back. He would not desire to have a better Radical at his back than Mr. Edward Evans. (Hear, hear.) And to-day it came out with the original remark that Mr. Moon had a little money to spend, rumour said it was £lOO, and he did not mind how he spent it. (Laughter.) When the Conservative press descended to that kind of argument, he thought they would know what value to place upon it. (Hear, hear.) He had no doubt that his opponent had as many thousands as he (Mr. Moon) had tens. (A voice "He is no better for that" and applause.) But it was not because he had earned it. kCrieers.) If he (Mr. Moon) owned the royalties that his opponent held, he would spend not £lOO but many a hundred. (Cheers.) That paper also said he was a stranger to Earlestown. He thought he might use the words of the Marquis of Salisbury and say ...I new nail that lie to the counter." tie had been visiting Ludes- town for the last 20 years,and he dared say, if the truth was known, he had been in it almost as often as hia opponent. (Hear, hear.) He knew enough about Earles- town to know what a pr)sperous and growing place it was. After his experience that week, and after seeing, those great works which went so far to maintain the town, the more he knew shout Earlestown the.better he liked it. (Hear, hear.) He was no pessimist. Ile be- lieved, in plain language, that there was a good time coming. He believed in the self government of the

-

===== 0002601_18950720_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
Ipeople, but the English speaking people weto n,t a self governing race. The 40 million people, might to-morrow pass a law unanimously and yet there was a house of hereditary legislators, which might thwart the measure. (Shame.) Why should men, just because they happen to be the sons of a rich brewer, govern them, and why should they have a birthright that the workingmen had net. (Applause.) There were a lot of measures that they were looking forward to. The measure of one man one vote (applause), which would put an end to the old teudal notion that property had more right to represen- tation than manhood. Then there was the great temper- ance question to which the Chairman had referred. He claimed that be was not an unreasonable man, and he should ,he sorry to support what he considered to be an unreasonable bill. But with regard to country public houses, where the Conservatives were trying to raise up a great agitation, he did not think the bill would affect country public houses, which found accommodation tor commercial travellers. What it aimed at was putting an end to those boozing dens in the large cities, where a man could not walk far without coming across a public house. They talked about robbing the poor man of his beer. He would like to know if Mr. Legh would like him to build a public-house opposite the gates of Lyme Hall, why should they have a public house right in front of their house? If the publican werea wise man,he would look at it in a diffent light to what he was doing. He believed that the best thing that could happen to the trade would be for the Government to pass the Bill and join with it the abolition of the tied house system. (Ap- plause.) Addresses were also delivered by Mr. E. Evans, Rev. E. Evans, Rev. Lloyd Jones, and Mr. H. Shawcross,and a resolution pledging the meeting to use all lawful means to return Mr. Moon was unanimously passed. Mr. Moon thanked them for the encouragement they had given him, and be believed his Tory friends were afraid the tide was leaving them, and if the Liberals of Newton were true to their promise on Monday they would have a Liberal member. (Cheers.) He con- cluded by moving a vote of thanks to the Chairman, which was heartily accorded.

LIBERAL MEETING AT MARTINSCROFT. On Saturday evening an open-air meeting of the sup- porters of Mr. James Moon, the Liberal candidate for the Newton Parliamentary division, was held on Martins- croft Green when Mr. J. T. Rowland occupied the chair, and the speeches were delivered from a lorry. Mr. J. Moon, the candidate, had been announced to speak, but he did not put in an appearance prior to the meeting dispersing, and it was explained that he had to address a meeting at Haydock at four o'clock and another at Earlestown at seven o'clock, and that no doubt he had been unable to reach Martinscroft in time. Mr. T. C. Lilley, of Manchester, spoke at some length and dealt with various items in the Liberal programme. Referring to local option, the speaker said the Conser- vatives seemed to be seriously alarmed at the question. He was not a teetotaler himself, and he did not think he was likely to become one, but he was in favour of Local Option. What did it mean? To-day a few magistrates could grant or withhold a license. The licensed vidualler was at the mercy of a few men who had in their power to either kill or cure. If he (the speaker) was a licensed 'victualler he should pray night and day for Local Option to be made law for h.s protection. He would be more secure in the hands of Itwo-thirds of the population of each vii laze and hamlet than of twelve men. Farmers need not be afraid of Local Option. It would not cause one ear- of barley less to be grown. They did not want to de- prive the workingman of his glass of beer. If the people wanted Local Option let them have it. As to the per- sonalities of the two candidates, Mr. Moen was born of a workingman's stock, had been a workingman himself, and by dint of enterprise had proved himself to be a business man by obtaining the position of Fellow of the , Institute of Actuaries, which proved that he was a man of learning. After having made some personal remarks as to Mr. Lee], he (Mr. Lilley) asked them to quit themselves like men, to strike for liberty, and let no one persuade them away, and whatever they did to vote for Mr. James Moon. (Applause.) Mr. Farnsworth, of the National Reform Union' also spoke, after which Mr. Bowleg proposed that Mr. James Moon was a fit and proper person to represent the elec- tors in Parliament. He had not heard Mr. Moon speak, but they had heard his friends, and if they acted as they preached they would " mend their country." (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. R. Gibbons seconded, and Mr. Richards supported the resolution. The latter remarked that Mr. Leigh had been in Parliament ten years, and he didn't see what he had done. He thought this time they ought to have a change. A change did good sometimes. He (the speaker) might tell them he was going to vote for "a staunch and true Radical." The resolution was put to the meeting and carried.

===== 0002601_18950720_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
LIBERAL MEETING AT NEWTON.

On Tuesday evening a meeting in support of Mr. Moon, the Liberal candidate for the Newton Division, was held in the Town Hall, Newton. There was a good audience, and Mr. Luffman presided. Amongst those present were Mr. James Moon, Mr. G. W. Rawlings,Mr. Owen, and others. The Chairman, at the outset, said he could not help reterring to a very sad event that had happened within the last few hours, and that had only just come to their gnowledge. An old candidate that they had there once, a very old friend, had gone to rest, Colonel Mc Corquodale was no more. He was sure he was expres- sing their deep sympathy with Colonel McCorquodale's family, as he was his own, in so very greatly deploring the sad event. (Hear, hear.) After referring to politi- cal topics, he called on Mr. Moon, who was cordially received. He said he was very glad to stand before the electors of Newton. He had been told again and again that it was a strong- hold of Toryism, and be was not dismayed on that account, because he must say that in that election both Conservatives and Liberals had given him a very fair hearing. He had nothing but gratitude to express for the reception he had met with. Be believed it was admitted that they were net relying on anything except the justice of their cause. (Cheers.) Men who relied on justice in their cause could always afford to discard everything else. He should discard the reference of his opponents to himself. He was not in the position Mr. Leh was in, hut if he were he would try tm set an example to his opponents, and he (Mr. Moon) would try to set it to him. (Applause.) There were one or two statements which had been published which it WAS his blenden duty not only to himself but to the cause he represented to correct. If they referred to the organs of Liberalism in the division they would find no insulting reference to their opponents. If they would refer to the organ of Conservatism they would find in it abuse. A few weeks ago it jeered at the idea that Mr. Moon was going to contest the Newton Division. Later it said Mr. Moon had been sent un to Newton Division by a Liverpool Radical, and it did not matter to him whether he won or lost. A fresh story WAS that Mr. Moon had a hundred pounds which he wanted to spend and was not particular how he spent it. (Laughter.) Then again they said the Roman Catholics were not going to support Mr. Moon because he was not in sympathy with their view on the education question. All these were pure inventions. Fle came there in his own right. (Cheers.) He had been selected after a fair amount of trial by the Liberal "300." (Applause.) Mr. Rawlines could bear out that months ago he said to him "Now I don't want to be invited to contest the divisor) unless I have the unanimous support of every part of the division." He was willing to address meetings so that the workingmen of Newton should say whether he was a fit and proper person to represent them. As the repre- sentative of the Liberals of that division he was just as proud of that position as the Conservative representive was of standing in the Conservative interest. (Applause.) He came there to advocate what he believed to be a very good cause. (Hear, hear.) The question before the country was a very anxious one. Many Liberals might he dismayed by the results of the elections on Saturday and Monday. Notwithstanding the fact that the Conservatives were being returned there was really a majority of votes against them. (Cheers.) Referring to Home Rule, he said he was as firm on Home Rule that day as he had ever been. He believed it was a question for the British working man to see that Ireland had Dome Rule, because if one part of the British Empire suffered every part suffered. (Hear. hear.) If they made Ireland nro.perous, they would be doing a great deal to mske England prosperous too. (Applause.) After dealing with the question of the Disestablishment of the Welsh Church. Mr. Moon said it had been given out that the Liberal party had done nothing within the last three rears. it wee the Liberal party which had given the Prieh Conneils and the Factory Act. They had regu- lated the hours of Railway Servants, and made working men Inspectors of Factories and Mines. After dealing with other prominent questions he urged them to go into ths contest as independent men, and if they wanted just equality they would vote for the Liberal candidate. (Cheers.) Mr. Owen moved a resolution to the effect that Mr. Moon was a fit and proper person to represent them in Parliament.

Mr. Borer seconded, and Mr. Aimlev, Mr. Burnish, and Mr. Rawlinqs concerted it by lensthy addrecses. The vet was tarried with only one dissmtient. A vote of thanks was afterwards accorded the Chair- man.

===== 0002601_18950720_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
CONSERVATIVE MEETING AT NEWTON.

A mein g in support of the candidature of the Tinn. T. W. Lfgh. wal held in the Town Hall, Newton-le- Willows on Monday evening. Mr. T. J. Gillespie. pre- sided, and he <vas supported no the nlatform by Messrs. T. D. GrirnkP-Dravton, W. Stone. R. Barton, C. B. F. Borron. W. Holland Owen, R. A. /Naylor (Warrington), and others.

The Chairman said ever7one would Agree with him that the present election was the most important they had been called upon tl fight. The question they had be- fore them was a simple one, but one that was, awfully important. What they had to ask themselves was were they to return Lord Salisbury to power with a good Unionist Government, and with such a majority that he would be able to carry nut the numerous useful matters of legislation which had been so ably brought before the public by Mr. Balfour, and other speakers, or were they

going to return a Separatist Government, under Lord Rosebery. ("so.") Mr. Barton moved a resolution of confidence in Mr. Legh, and pledging the meeting to use all lawful en. deavours to return him to Parliament on Monday next- Mr. T. D. Grimke-Drayton seconded. Mr. R. A. Naylor, of Warrington, in the couree of his observations read extracts from last week's " Examiner" and ridiculed the idea that Mr. Legh's majority of 894 would be wiped out, lie claimed to be a temperance man, and said he was perfectly disgusted at the way the temperance people were treating Mr Legh over this job, and it was just on a line with all their other dirty work. He bad been going at the temperance question a great number of years. Mr.Legh in his address mentioned the question of temperance reform, and Mr. Moon grumbled at Mr. Legh for men- tioning temperance reform, and not telling him what he was going to do. The great Liberal party for a number of years had come forward and said they must have the temperance vote, as they were the temperance party. He once voted for a Liberal candidate on the strength of the temperance question but it was the last time. He had been fooled and humbugged once, but never again. (Laughter.) The man he voted for turned out to be fond of whiskey and water. (Laughter.) He was willing that some Act should be passed, so that the magistrates should have the power to close certain useless and objectionable public houses, but at the same time give compensation to those who had invested their money in the business, and the ratepayers would have no need to be taxed to the extent of a penny. (Applause.) Mr. Legh in the course of a brief address, said be did not consider he was fighting a candidate at all, but the Prudential Assurance Society. His opponent relied not so much upon his power ef argument, as on the discip- line of a well-meaning phalanx of canvassers, who went about the division asking the electors to vote for Mr. Moon. With regard to the question of temperance re- form, he was not a minister, and was not responsible for the inauguration of the policy in the House of Commons. He followed the lead of Lord Salisbury and Mr. Balfour, and he was sufficienlly modest to think that if he pro- duced a temperance programme of his own, and laid down certain conditions, which he thought ought to be adhered to, it was just possible that Lord Salisbury might not adopt it. But far the benefit of Mr. Moon and his allies, he did not intend to expound a temper- ance policy in all its bearings. He believed in compen- sation, and they would not attain any satisfactory result until they admitted that. (Applause.) The resolution was then put and carried and the meet- ing terminated.

===== 0002601_18950720_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
CONSERVATIVE MEETING AT WARRINGTON.

A meeting in support of the Hon. T. W. Legh's can- didature for the Newton Division was held on Wednes- day afternoon in the Assembly Room of the Warrington Conservative Club. Alderman Francomb presided, and amongst those present were Mr. Robert Pierpoint, M.P., the Hon. T. W. Legh, the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, Aldermen Bolton and Sutton, Councillors Bennett, R. T. Fairclough, Hepherd, Longton, Skelton, Hill, Shaw and Lee, and Messrs. Robert Davies, John White, R. I. Wynne-Jones, D. W. Jeans, John Fairclough, Mather, T. Halton, William Gill (Winmarleigh-at.), James Ford, A. Jolley, W. J. Forshaw, W. H. J. Jenkins, Royston, D. Jones,T. D.Grimke-Dravton, T.Brookes,W. W. Westwell, and Owen (Mr. Leigh's election agent). There was a good attendance. The Chairman said they all knew what a glorious vic- tory the Conservatives had in Warrington on Monday last, and he hoped the same enthusiasm would be dis- played by Mr. Legh's supporters on Monday next. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Allusion was made by the Chairman to local option and its effects, and he observed that in Bootle,where there are only between one and two public-houses per 1,000 persons, there were 24, persons per 1,000 convicted of drunkenness. In Newcastle-en- Tyne, where there were only between three and four public-houses per 1.000, there were 26 convictions per 1,000 persons. In Liverpool, with only four public- houses per 1,000, there were 16 convictions per 1,000 In Ryde, where there are nine public-houses per 1,000 persons, there were only between one and two convic- tions per 1.000, and in Winchester, where there are eight public-houses per 1,000 persons, there were only be- tween one and two convictions per 1,000 persons. These were facts which should be remembered by all of them. Mr. Robert Davies, who described himself as " one of the ancients," said he was waiting very anxiously to see the result of the turn of the tide which was flowing with the Conservatives at the present moment. What had become of all the vapouring on the Radical side in claim- ing to have the people with them universally l (Laugh- ter.) He (Mr. Davies) believed that the people were with the Conservatives. They saw the folly and waste of time which had been occupied in discussing questions which had never been carried int. effect. What they wanted as a people was peace and domestic legislation. (Applause.) He moved "That this meeting welcomes the dissolution of Parliament,and sincerely hopes for the accession of a strong Unionist Government to power; and, while thanking Mr. Legh for his able representa- tion of the Newton Division, pledges itself to use all legitimate means to secure his triumphant return at the coming pie tion." (Applause.) Mr. T. D. Grimke Drayton seconded the motion which was sup; orted by Mr. Jeaos, Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins, and the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton.

The motion was supported by Mr. Robert Pierpoint, M.P., and unanimously agreed te. Mr. Legh, who was received with applause, congratu- lated Mr. Pierpoint upon his success at the poll on Mon- day at Warrington, and the electors upon having given ons more blow in defence of the Conservative and Unionist cause. He might be wrong, but be was under the impression that the voters generally were dissatisfied with every part of the policy of the late Government. The Government had dragged on a miserable existence for three years without governing the nation properly, but what could they expect from a party which cor sisted of a collection of discordant groups, each of which claimed for its policy to he carried into effect first ? The policy of the Liberals was not dictated by a desire to promote the welfare of the country, but it was a policy which was dictated by sections in turn,and he contended that such a Government was not likely to receive much attention at the hands of intelligent voters. (Applause.) On the motion of Alderman Sutton, seconded by Mr. Owen, a vote oF thanks was passed to Alderman Fran- i cemb for presiding, and the meeting terminated.

• CONSERVATIVE MEE LING AT EARLESTOWN.

A largely attended meeting of Conservatives in sup- port of tbe candidature of the Hon. T. W. Legh, was held in the Town Hall, Earlitown, on Thursday evening. Mr. C. B. F. Borron presided, and he was supported by Lord Balcarres, M.P., the Hon. T. W. Legh, Rev. T. R. Matthews, Colonel W. H. Morrison, Messrs. T. J. Gillespie, W. H. Owen, T. D. Grimke-Drayton, Dr. Handyside, Dr. Patterson, Mrs. Legh, Mrs. Gillespie and other ladies and gentlemen. The Chairman said before commencing the proceedings, he had to refer, to the great loss they had sustained in the death of Colonel McCorquodale. He was known to them all, as one who had worked diligently in local affairs, as chairman of Vie old Commissioners, and as chairman )f the sessions, and in the county as deputy lieutenant, and having occupied the position of high sheriff he would be much missed in the district. (Hear, hear.) The Hon. T.W. Legh said he was inclined to think that when he first appeared before them as a candidate, he made the greatest mistake that a politician could make. He was far too modest, but he was not going to make that mistake again, and he was going boldly to announce that he considered himself as well fitted to represent them as Mr. Moon, and that if there was a little more prudence in Mr. Moon's nature, he would not come forward to contest that seat.— A resolution pledging the meeting to return Mr. Legh at the head of the poll was moved by Mr. James Smith, and seconded by Dr. Handyside, and supported by Colonel Morrison and Lord Balcarres, and afterwards unanimously carried.

THE GENERAL ELECTION.

The state of parties up to Friday morning was as fol- lows :—Liherals, 76 ; ConsPrvatives, 262; Liberal Union- ists, 43 ; Nationalists, 38 ; Fameßites, six. As the losses count 110 on a division and the Home Rule majority at the dissolution was 28. the Unionists have now a majority in the new House ot 82.

THE SHOOTING AT BISLEY.

The second stage of the Queen's Prize competition was completed at Risley on Thursday. Sergeant W. nogg, of the Border Rifles, won the silver medal with an aggregate of 205 points. Private Robinson, 2nd Man- chester, was second with 204, and Armoury-Sergeant Fraser, Queen's Edinburgh, third with 203. There was some remarkable shooting in the " Graphic " (M. 11. competition (seven shots at 500 yards). No fewer than 24 marksmen recorded highest possibles, and on shoot- ing off the ties seven of them put on successive bulls with their three shots, while Ave had totals of 14, and eight totals of 13. Private WGibbon, 3rd Lanark, and , Lance-Corporal Wilson, let Dunbarton, tied for the " Daily Telegraph" Cop.

LOCAL SUCCESS. Mr. Wilding, the assistant surveyor at Runcorn, Lai been successful at the recent examination of the City and Guilds of London, in obtaining first honours. He is to be congratulated upon the progress he is making in his profession. Already he is the possessor of many honour- able distinctions. He holds a first honour certificate ; a bronze medal for brickwork and masonry ; and a certifi- cate as well as silver medal for plasterers' work and build- ing construction.

Boy DROWNED IN THD SHIP CANAL.

On Thursday evening Joseph Dunbavand, aged 15 years. was playing on the banks of the Ship Canal flair Runcorn lay-by, when be slipped and fell in. One of his companions, George Kinsey, tried to reach him, but without avail. Before further assistance arrives he sank. Dragging operations were immediately com- menced, but without success.

p ARLIAMENTARY ELECTION, 1895. TO THE ELECTORS OF THE NEWTON DIVISION OF LANCASHIRE. GENTLEMEN,— Having been invited by the Newton Liberal Association to contest the Seat of the forthcoming Election, I willingly place myself in your hands, relying on the earnest promises of support received from every part of the Division. I do not desire to put forth any personal claim to the honour of securing your votes, although I can claim an extensive experience and heartfelt sympathy with the work- ing classes—amongst whom my life has been spent from the day I worked at the bench as an apprentice—but I do feel that the cause I represent is one which entitles me to the support of all earnest reformers, and especially of every workingman in the Newton Division. A Dissolution being imminent, every Liberal will rejoice at the opportunity of ascertaining the will of the people upon the great questions with which the late Government was Identified. Unfortunately in this Division the plural voter— thanks to the Conservative party—has another lease of life and another opportunity of neutralizing the wishes of the resident voters, making it impossible to know with certainty what those wishes really are; we believe them to be in sympathy with theLlberal party, and I feel It an honour to champion them in this Contest. The issue before the country has been clearly and well de- fined as :- I.—lrish Self-Government or Home Rule. 2.—Social and Political Reform 3.—The Supremacy of the House of Commons. In all of these the late Government has, in the face of bitter opposition and hatred, struggled to voice what we be- lieve to be the will ot the people. 12As a result, we now have the Parish Councils Act, giving local self-government to every parish, the death duties and the Income Tax have been readjusted, the Hours of Railway Servants have been regulated; and in in its administration the Government has shown its practical sympathy with Labour by adopting the Eight-hours Day and the Trades Union Rate of Waves, by raising the minimum weekly wage os their labourers, appointing Workingmen Magistrates, Fac- tory Inspectors, ko. Unfortunately, the House of Lords, with its hereditary class legislators, has stepped in to thwart the will of the House of Commons by destroying the Llome Rule Bill. the Employers' Liability Bill, and an Irish Bill for Evicted Tenants. ,2 The electoral of Newton havernow an opportunity of ex- pressing their opinion on this unexampled record of work done, and approving or otherwise, the action of the House of Lords. If a Conservative majority in the next Parliament be obtained, the tide of grogress will be stemmed, and much needed legislative reforms deferred indefinitely. Should. however, the Liberals be returned with an increased majority, we shall enter upon a new era of social and political reform and progress which can only result In the betterment and increased happiness of the people. I refer to such important questions as the Reform of the Land Laws in the interests of Agriculture, Popular Control of the Liquor Traffic, Payment of Members, One Man One Vote, Welsh Disestablishment, Old Age Pensions, Taxation of Ground Values and Mining Royalties, Poor-law Reform, and the Abolition of the Veto of tke House of Lords, with all of which I am in hearty sympathy. I appeal to you in confidence to refuse your support to a party which is stnwilllng or unable to openly declare its policy and I ask you to return me to the House of Commons to support the principles which I have already publicly advocoted, and in support of which I hope shortly to address you. I am, Gentlemen. Your obedient servant, JAMES MOON. Liverpool, July Ist, 1895.

WHAT BRAVE MEN ENDURE WHEN THE SMOKE OF THE BATTLE IS DISSOLVED. Friendships made on the battle-field survive the fight- ing, and brave soldiers are always eager to rescue com- rades from the perils that outlive the wars. The bond between comrade and comrade is stronger than steel. They treasure the memories of the time they stood shoulder to shoulder, and they do not forget that the danger to soldiers does not end when the bullets ceaoe to fly ; that regiments are ill and suffering to-day ~L use of the exposure to cold and wet and nervous excitement they had to undergo, or that in these campaigns more brave lads are stricken by disease than by lead or steel. When a veteran finds a road to health he embraces every opportunity to tell his comrades, and that is why Major McMurray, Springfield House, Killuney, Armagh, wrote the following letter :—" I was a martyr to rheumatic gout from 1865 until April, 1888, being laid up with it regularly every six months, sometimes for weeks and sometimes for months. In April, 1888, I commenced to take Warner's Safe Cure. I found relief after the fourth bottle. I took twenty-one bottles. Since I stopped taking the medicine in July, 1888, I have not had the slightest indications of rheumatic gout." PENKETH. CHURCH LADS' BRIGADE.—On Sunday the St. Mary's Company of the C.L.B.assembled at the Mission Sunday School, and were marched up to St. Mary's Church. There was a strong muster,and the command was taken by Captain McKechnie. The brass band headed the pro- cession ,and a large number of villagers followed them to church, where a service was held, the Rev. J. R. Jones conducting. A collection in aid of the brigade funds was taken and ameunted to over £4 10s. On Tuesday evening the Company was paraded on the Recreation Ground, and was joined by the companies of St. Elphin's, Padgate and St. Peter's. Major Buckton was in command, and the combined companies were put through several difficult evolutions, which they per- formed in a very creditable manner. Captain McKechnie and Lieutenant Appleton were also pleeent,and the bat- talion at the close of the review did the "March Past " in a very soldierly manner. The Recreation Ground was well filled by an interested crowd of spectators. The review ended at 9.15, and the Warrington companies departed in 'busses. The band was in attendance and enlivened the proceedings at intervals by the rendering of lively marches, Im. BERRETTA WESLEYAN BRASS BAND.—On Tuesday even- ing the above band paraded the village under the con- ductorship of Mr. J. Bowyer. The band marched round by Messrs. Garnett's Works and up through the village and the music was much appreciated by the villagers.

A RAILWAY MA NAG ER'S BROAD HINT. A few months ago the son of an-American millionaire railway director was, through his father's influence, given a position of some importance on one of the largest railways in the West. fie had just graduated from college, and in the orders which he from time to time issued to the men under him always made use of the longest, most unus at words. This habit led to some rather expensive blunders,and the matter corning before the general manager, he suggested a change in sts le to the young official in the following letter :—" In promul- gating your esoteric cogitations, and in articulating your superficial sentimentalities and amicable philosophical or psychological observations, beware of platitudinous ponderosity. Let your conversational communication possess a clarified conciseness, a com- pacted comprehensibleness,a coalescent consistency, and a concatenated cogency. Eschew all conglomeration of flatulent garrulity, jejune babbleinentand asinine affec- tation. Let your extt mprraneous descantings and un- premediated expatiation have Intelligibility and veraci- ous vivacity without rhodomontade or thrasonical born- bast. Sedulously avoid all polysyllabic profundity-, pompous prolixity, psittaceous vacuity, ver,triol quial verbosity, and vandoquent vapidity. Shun double en- tendre, prurient jocosity, and pestiferous profanity, obscurant or apparent. In other words talk plaMly, briefly, naturally, sensibly, purely and truthfully. Keep from slang ; don't put on airs ; say what you mean ; mean what you say ; and don't use big words." The young official took the gentle hint and changed his style.

A STARTLING GAS-BURNER.—When gas was first intro- dwied it was thought wonderful that a flame could be kept alight without a wick, but the latest invention in gas-burners quite eclipses this, as it not only burns the gas, but actually makes the gas light itself. This won- , demi invention,patented by Mr J. F. Duke, of 3,Union- square, London, RE., and wild by the Duke Patents Company, 20, High Holborn, is pronounced to be one of the most marvellous subjugations of science to domestic economy. It consists of just an ordinary gas-burner with a small tube at the side. This burner is simply screwed into the bracket or other fitting, the gas turned ' on, and lo ! in from 6 to 10 seconds (for the air in the pipe to pass out), the gas lights itself. The action of this burner is beautifully simple,and is as follows ;—The gas on issuing from the burner passes over a small kredi of porals material impregnated with a rare and indestructible metal, which as soon as the gas passes over it becomes red hot; this red heat is communicated to a small piece of platinum wire which immediately becomes incandescent and lights the gas. This little black knob is practically indestructible as is also the platinum wire. Therefore, as long as the burner exists, the gas on being turned on will light itself. The burner and lighter is of the ordinary size, and will fit any exist- ing fitting ; and although the price (23. 6d.) may at first seem large it will very soon repay itself, asfrona 20 to 40 per cent, of the gas bill is guaranteed to be saved and risk of fire greatly reduced. The editor of the Journal of Gas-lighting of June 25th, says :—"So beautifully simple and effective an appliance argues a long and arduous process of development. . The possible range of utility of :gr. Duke's invention is inarnenie.' In 1884, the world produced 768,000 tons of tobacco. grown on 2,029,000 acres of rn n . The United States was the largest prcd tiger, 210 000 tons; next cam' India, with 170,000 ; Russia, w,rii 75,000, Ao w:ti 65,000; the remainder being, produced in mall quantities by other countries.
